# Changelog

All notable changes to this project will be documented in this file.

## [Unreleased]

### Added

- **Mount an API in the Django URLconf: `path("blog/", api.urls)`** - `api.urls` gives the URLconf module and the namespaces of a `BoltAPI`, as `admin.site.urls` does. Bolt reads the URLconf one time at startup. It serves each route at the path that `reverse()` gives: the prefixes of each enclosing `include()`, then the route path. Thus `reverse("blog:api:detail")` works through nested namespaces, and the served path is the same. A route that the API copied with `mount()` also gets the namespace of each mounted API, for example `blog:api:<namespace>:<name>`. WebSocket routes, native routes, ASGI mounts, MCP servers and the docs move under the prefix too. A `str` parameter in the prefix is in `request.params`. `runbolt` and the test clients refuse a prefix that Bolt cannot serve with `ImproperlyConfigured`, and the message names the URLconf entry. Examples are `re_path()`, `i18n_patterns()`, other converters, two parameters in one segment, one parameter two times, and a prefix with no trailing slash. They also refuse an ASGI mount, a Django mount or an MCP server under a prefix with a parameter. Django alone does not serve Bolt routes, so `manage.py check`, `migrate` and `reverse()` accept such a URLconf. The exception is one parameter name two times in a path: there `reverse()` raises the error of Django itself. `runbolt` and the test clients serve a mounted API one time, under its prefixes only. An API that the URLconf does not mount serves at the same paths as before. `django_bolt.urls` leaves out a mounted API. See [Mount an API in the URLconf](docs/src/topics/routing.md#mount-an-api-in-the-urlconf). (#552)
- **`FORCE_SCRIPT_NAME`: serve a project under a sub-path** - Behind a proxy that removes a sub-path, Bolt now gives the paths that Django gives under WSGI. `request.path` starts with the script name, and the new `request.path_info` does not. `request.META["SCRIPT_NAME"]` is the setting. `request["path"]`, `get_full_path()`, `build_absolute_uri()`, the `Location` of a trailing-slash redirect, the default OpenAPI server and the schema URL of the docs pages start with it. `WebSocket.path` starts with it too, the new `WebSocket.path_info` does not, and the scope of a WebSocket route has it in `root_path`. `reverse()` and `get_script_prefix()` give it in handlers, dependencies, middleware, error formatters, streaming bodies, WebSocket handlers, MCP tools, resources and prompts, ASGI mounts, lifespans, `sync_to_thread()` and `run_in_orm_executor()`. Bolt sets it for each call and puts the previous value back, so the other state of a thread, such as a cache backend of Django, stays as without the setting. The `root_path` of the scope of an ASGI mount starts with it. Django takes the setting in place of the mount path of `mount_django()`, as its `ASGIHandler` does. Thus the URL patterns of such an app contain the mount path, as with `clear_root_path=True`, and Bolt does not change the `Location` of its redirects. Bolt routes the path without the script name, as Django's `WSGIHandler` does, and the `exclude_paths` of a middleware match that path. `runbolt` and the test clients refuse some values at startup, before a lifespan runs. See the entry under Changed. Without the setting, nothing changes. Before, `SCRIPT_NAME` was always empty, and `reverse()` in a handler gave paths without the sub-path. See [Serve under a sub-path](docs/src/topics/deployment.md#serve-under-a-sub-path). (#552)
- **bolt-mcp: the MCP Tasks extension (SEP-2663)** - `@mcp.tool(task=True, ttl_ms=..., poll_interval_ms=...)` runs a call as a task. A 2026-07-28 client that declares `io.modelcontextprotocol/tasks` on its request gets `resultType: "task"` at once, and the tool runs on. The client reads the state and the result with `tasks/get`, answers input requests with `tasks/update` and stops the task with `tasks/cancel`. Each other client gets the tool result as before. `tasks/cancel` and the end of the TTL cancel the coroutine of an `async def` tool. A `def` tool runs in a thread, which runs to its end. In a task, `ctx.elicit()` waits for `tasks/update` and does not run the tool again. `ctx.report_progress()` and log lines set the `statusMessage`, and `ctx.task_id` gives the task id. Only the principal that created a task can reach it. Another caller gets the error of an unknown task id. Bolt keeps a finished task for one more TTL. `MCP(max_tasks=1000)` limits the tasks of a mount, running and finished. `MCP(max_tasks_per_principal=100)` limits the tasks of one caller. `SIGTERM` cancels each running task and waits up to 5 seconds for its cleanup. Tasks live in the memory of one process. Thus `runbolt` refuses to start when a mount has a task tool and `--processes` is above 1. It also refuses with `--max-rss` or `--workers-lifetime`. (#299)
- **More standard containers in a response are JSON** - `return {"ids": range(3)}` gave a 500 with `TypeError: Unsupported type`. Now a `ChainMap`, a `MappingProxyType` and a `UserDict` are a JSON object. A `range`, an `array.array`, a dict view, a `UserList`, a `map` object and a `zip` object are a JSON array. A `UserString` is a string. The items go through the encoder again. A `ZoneInfo`, a `timezone`, a `SimpleNamespace` and a `complex` have no clear JSON form, so they still give a 500, as with `DjangoJSONEncoder`. The docs now list the types that a response can contain. See [Values in a JSON response](docs/src/topics/responses.md#values-in-a-json-response). A typed response validates first, so a value must match the declared type. For example, `-> list[int]` refuses a `range`. (#875)
- **bolt-mcp: `@mcp.tool_middleware`** - An async function runs around each `tools/call` that passes authentication and the guards of the tool. Bolt rejects a call to an unknown or denied tool in Rust, before middleware runs, so middleware does not see these calls. The function gets a `ToolCall` with the tool name, the raw arguments, the request `_meta` and the principal, and `call_next()` returns the `CallToolResult` dict. Bolt adds `resultType` to a result from middleware that does not have it. MCP calls do not pass through HTTP middleware, so this is the place for per-call logging, metrics and tracing, with trace context from `_meta` (`traceparent`). Before, a tool read `_meta` only through a `Context` parameter. (#491)
- **bolt-mcp: `@mcp.resource_middleware` and `@mcp.prompt_middleware`** - The same kind of middleware runs around every `resources/read` and `prompts/get`. It gets a `ResourceRead` (URI) or `PromptGet` (prompt name and raw arguments), each with the request `_meta` and the principal. Before, Rust passed only the URI, or the name and arguments, to Python. A resource or prompt could not see the trace context or the caller. Now Rust passes `_meta` and the principal to Python for every `tools/call`. It passes them for a `resources/read` or a `prompts/get` only when middleware for it exists. To refuse a read or a prompt, raise the new `bolt_mcp.JsonRpcError`. The client then gets a JSON-RPC error with your message. Register middleware before `mount_mcp`. Registration after the mount raises `RuntimeError`. (#491)
- **bolt-mcp: OpenTelemetry spans with `bolt_mcp.otel.instrument(mcp)`** - Install `bolt-mcp[otel]` and call `instrument(mcp)`. Each `tools/call`, `resources/read` and `prompts/get` then gets one `SERVER` span, for example `tools/call plan_journey`. The parent of the span is the `traceparent` and `tracestate` in the request `_meta`. The span has the attributes of the OpenTelemetry semantic conventions for MCP. A failed call sets the `ERROR` status and `error.type`. Bolt changes a tool exception into an `isError` result first, so a tool gives `tool_error`. A resource or prompt exception gives the class name of the exception. The span is the current span while the tool runs, so a span that a sync or async tool makes is its child. The span records the tool arguments, the tool result, the exception text and the query values of a resource URI only when `OTEL_INSTRUMENTATION_GENAI_CAPTURE_MESSAGE_CONTENT=true`. Without it, each query value in `mcp.resource.uri` is `REDACTED`, and the fragment is removed. The tool name, the prompt name and the URI path always go in the span. `mcp.resource.uri` never has userinfo. `instrument()` registers middleware, so middleware that you register after it runs in the span. A second `instrument()` call on one server raises `ValueError`. `bolt_mcp` does not import OpenTelemetry, so a server that does not call `instrument()` does not change. (#491)
- **bolt-mcp: `ToolError` and `MCP(mask_error_details=...)`** - A tool raises `ToolError` for a message the client may read. Bolt now masks the text of any other exception from a tool, resource or prompt. The client gets a fixed message, for example `Tool 'name' failed.`, and Bolt logs the full traceback on the server. **Behavior change:** before, the client got the exception text. This text can contain database URLs, passwords and file paths. Before, a tool exception was not logged, and a resource or prompt exception was logged by Rust without a traceback. To send the exception text to the client during development, use `MCP(mask_error_details=False)`. When the client cannot answer, `ctx.elicit()` and `ctx.sample()` now raise `McpClientError` in place of `RuntimeError`. Thus their message still reaches the client. (#492)
- **bolt-mcp: `ToolResult`** - A tool can return `ToolResult(content=..., structured=..., meta=...)` to set its text content apart from its `structuredContent`. `content` is a string or a list of MCP content blocks, and `meta` becomes the result's `_meta`. Other return values map as before, with the JSON of the structured content as the text. `output_schema="auto"` on a tool that returns `ToolResult`, or a union that contains `ToolResult`, raises `TypeError`. Bolt does not validate `structured` against the `output_schema` of the tool. (#490)
- **bolt-mcp: `@mcp.resource(meta=...)`** - The dict is sent as `_meta` on the `resources/read` contents, for static resources and templates. MCP Apps read the content security policy and other iframe settings of an app from it (`ui.csp`, `ui.domain`, `ui.prefersBorder`). Before, resource contents had no `_meta`. (#489)
- **bolt-mcp: `@mcp.tool(meta=...)`** - The dict is sent as the tool's `_meta` in `tools/list`. MCP Apps read the tool's UI from it (`ui.resourceUri`), and `ui.visibility` hides a tool from the model. Before, a tool had no `_meta`. (#488)
- **A database store for `JWTAuthViews`: `django_bolt.auth.revocation_db`** - Bolt now ships the table for `DjangoORMRevocation`. Add `"django_bolt.auth.revocation_db"` to `INSTALLED_APPS` and run `migrate`. Then `DjangoORMRevocation()` with no `model` uses it. Before, each project had to write its own model. `DjangoORMRevocation` also had no `get_user_version` or `bump_user_version`, so `JWTAuthViews` gave a 500 on each login with a database store. Now the store keeps the version of a user in the row `version:<user_id>`. One `UPDATE` adds 1 to the version, so concurrent bumps do not lose a count. The row does not expire, so the cleanup task of expired rows keeps it. A model of your own still works with `model=`, if it has the fields of the shipped model, including `version`.
- **Native routes** - `api.native_route(method, path, capsule)` serves a route with a Rust handler from another extension module, without Python. See [Native Routes](docs/src/topics/native-routes.md).
- **`@cors(origin_regexes=[...])`** - A route can allow the origins that match a pattern, as `CORS_ALLOWED_ORIGIN_REGEXES` does for all routes. `@cors` needs `origins` or `origin_regexes`. An empty pattern raises `ValueError`, because it matches every origin. An origin or a pattern that is not a `str` raises `TypeError`.
- **`field()` options in the OpenAPI schema** - `field(description=, title=, examples=, deprecated=, include_in_schema=)` of a `Serializer` had no effect. Now the property schema has `description`, `title`, `examples` and `deprecated: true`. `include_in_schema=False` leaves the property out of the schema and out of `required`. The field still validates input and is still in the output. The options apply to request body schemas, response schemas and nested serializers.
- **Computed fields in the OpenAPI schema** - The response schema of a `Serializer` has a property for each `@computed_field`. The property is required and has `readOnly: true`, and its type is the return type of the method. `@computed_field(description=, deprecated=, include_in_schema=)` had no effect. Now `description` (or else the docstring of the method), `deprecated` and the new `title` and `examples` arguments are in the property schema, and `include_in_schema=False` leaves the property out. A request body schema has no computed field. The response schema has no `write_only` field and no field with `exclude=True`, because `dump()` does not return them. The request body schema marks a `write_only` field `writeOnly: true`.
- **`deprecated=True` on a route** - The route decorators of `BoltAPI` and `Router`, `@api.view()`, `@api.viewset()` and `@action` take `deprecated`. The OpenAPI operation of such a route gets `deprecated: true`. The server continues to serve the route. An operation that is not deprecated has no `deprecated` key. Before, each operation had `"deprecated": false`. See [Deprecating endpoints](docs/src/topics/openapi.md#mark-a-route-as-deprecated).
- **`request.scheme` and `request.is_secure()`** - The Bolt request gives its scheme, as a Django request does. `request.scheme` is `"http"` or `"https"`, and `request.is_secure()` is `True` for `"https"`. They give the scheme of `request.build_absolute_uri()`: the scheme follows `BOLT_TRUSTED_PROXIES`, and `SECURE_PROXY_SSL_HEADER` applies on top of it. Bolt finds the value on the first read, so a handler that does not read it pays nothing. See [Scheme](docs/src/topics/requests.md#build-absolute-urls).
- **`TemplateResponse` and `process_template_response`** - A handler can return a `TemplateResponse` from `django_bolt.responses`. This is the `TemplateResponse` class of Django. Bolt renders it after the handler. Before the render, a route behind `DjangoMiddleware` or `DjangoMiddlewareStack` calls `process_template_response` of each Django middleware, the last one first, as Django does. A hook can change `template_name` and `context_data`. `process_response` then gets the rendered response. A `TemplateResponse` from `process_view` or `process_exception` goes through the same hooks. A hook can be `async`. A hook that returns `None` raises `ValueError`, and `process_exception` does not get an error of a hook, as in Django. The hooks and the render run where a sync ORM call can run, so a template can read a QuerySet. Before, Bolt did not run this hook, and a `TemplateResponse` from a handler gave a 500. See [Template responses](docs/src/topics/responses.md#render-a-template-later). (#868)

### Fixed

- **The rate limiter deletes full buckets first** - Each process keeps up to 100,000 buckets for all routes. Above that number, Bolt deleted 20% of the buckets in no fixed order, also buckets that held state. A client whose bucket was deleted got its full burst again. Now Bolt first deletes each bucket that is full again. Such a bucket holds no state, so this changes no limit. Only when these are fewer than 20% of the buckets does Bolt delete other buckets, up to 20%. The request path does not change.
- **`auth_claims` has a valid `is_admin`** - A token with `"is_admin": true` or `false` gave no `is_admin` key in `request.context["auth_claims"]`, while a malformed `is_admin` was there as `None`. `Token.create(is_admin=True)` writes this claim, so a handler that read `auth_claims["is_admin"]` got `KeyError`. Now `auth_claims` has it, as it has `is_staff`. `Requires("is_admin")` read the claim before too.
- **The docs say what `Requires("permissions")` with no values needs** - The docs said that a claim with no values must be present and not `null`. For `permissions`, Bolt needs one permission or more. A JWT with `"permissions": []`, an API key with no permissions and a session user with no permissions get 403. The docs now say so. Bolt does not change.
- **The cookie CSRF check refuses a port that is not a number in range** - With no `Sec-Fetch-Site`, the check compares the `Origin` with the scheme and host of the request. When the request has no `Origin`, it compares the `Referer`. It read a port such as `99999`, `65536`, `1a` or `+443`, and text between an IPv6 host and its port, as the default port. Thus `Origin: https://example.com:99999` passed for `Host: example.com`, and `Host: example.com:99999` passed with `Origin: https://example.com`. Now such a port fails the check, as it fails a URL in a browser (WHATWG URL). **Behavior change:** when the cookie CSRF check runs, an unsafe request now gets 403 if `Host` or the header that the check compares (`Origin`, else `Referer`) has such a port.
- **A cookie `expires` with a time zone is sent in UTC** - Bolt wrote the clock time of an aware `datetime` with "GMT", and ignored its time zone. Thus a Tokyo time of 12:00 went out as 12:00 GMT, 9 hours late, and a New York time went out 5 hours early. A cookie for one hour from `timezone.localtime()` in the Americas arrived expired. Now Bolt converts an aware `datetime` to UTC, as Django's `set_cookie` does (RFC 9110, section 5.6.7). A naive `datetime` is UTC, as before. `Cookie.to_header_value()` changes in the same way. **Behavior change:** the `Expires` of an aware `datetime` that is not in UTC is now the correct UTC time.
- **A cookie path, domain or expiry date cannot add an attribute** - Bolt checked the name and the value of a cookie, but not its `path`, `domain` and `expires`. A `;` in one of them added an attribute after the attributes of Bolt. A line break in `path` gave a 500 for the whole response, and a tab went out in the header. Now Bolt does not send a cookie with a `;` or a control character in these fields, and logs a warning, as for a bad value (RFC 6265, section 4.1.1). Django refuses a control character in these fields too. **Behavior change:** such a cookie is not sent. The rest of the response goes out.
- **`samesite="None"` no longer adds `Secure`** - With `samesite="None"` and `secure=False`, Bolt sent `Secure`. The library that builds the header adds it for `SameSite=None`. Django and `Cookie.to_header_value()` send no `Secure` then. Now Bolt sends `Secure` only for `secure=True`. **Behavior change:** a cookie with `samesite="None"` and the default `secure=False` has no `Secure` now. A browser refuses a `SameSite=None` cookie without `Secure`, so pass `secure=True` with `samesite="None"`.
- **JWT: a JWKS key that Bolt cannot use no longer fails the key set** - Bolt refused the whole key set when one key was of a type that Bolt does not support, missed a member, or was not a JSON object. An example is an `X25519` key for encryption. With `jwks_url` or `oidc_issuer`, the server did not start. When the provider added such a key later, each refresh failed, and Bolt kept the old keys. After the next key rotation, each token of the new key got 401. Now Bolt skips each such key, as RFC 7517, section 5 asks, and PyJWT does the same. A key set with no usable key still stops the startup.
- **JWT: only the signing keys of a JWKS verify a token** - Bolt used each key of a key set to verify tokens, also a key with `"use": "enc"`. Keycloak, for example, publishes an `RSA-OAEP` encryption key next to its signing key. Now Bolt skips a key whose `use` is not `sig`, as `PyJWKClient` does (RFC 7517, section 4.2). A key with no `use` still verifies. **Behavior change:** a token signed with a key whose `use` is not `sig` now gets 401, and a key set with only such keys stops the startup.
- **A Django mount keeps a path that starts with its mount path again** - With `api.mount_django("/legacy")`, a request to `/legacy/legacy/items` gave Django the path `/legacy/items`, so Django routed `/items`. Now Django gets `/legacy/legacy/items` and routes `/legacy/items`, as for each other path under the mount. (#552)
- **`DJANGO_BOLT_MAX_PARAM_LENGTH` counts the bytes that the client sent** - Bolt checked a multipart text field on its bytes, and then again on its decoded text. A byte that is not UTF-8 becomes U+FFFD, which has three bytes. Thus 17 bytes `0xFF` with a limit of 17 got 422 with "Parameter too long: 51 bytes". A query value and a URL-encoded form value had the same fault for an escape such as `%FF`. Now a query or URL-encoded value counts its bytes after the percent-decoding. A multipart value counts its bytes as sent. The check runs once, and the 422 message gives that count. A path, header or cookie value does not change, because its text is never longer than the bytes that the client sent. (audit finding F8)
- **CORS sends `Vary: Origin` on each response that depends on the `Origin`** - With a list of origins, a pattern, or a wildcard with credentials, only a response to an allowed origin had `Vary: Origin`. A response to a refused origin, or to a request with no `Origin`, had none. Thus a shared cache could give such a response to a CORS request of an allowed origin, and the browser blocked it. Now each response of a route that this config covers has `Vary: Origin`. This includes errors, redirects, streams, files, static files and ASGI mounts. Caches can now tell the responses apart. A preflight to a refused origin now also has `Vary: Access-Control-Request-Method, Access-Control-Request-Headers`, because the requested method selects the route config. Bolt adds each name one time. It keeps the `Vary` values of the handler, in any letter case, and it does not change `Vary: *`. With `CORS_ALLOW_ALL_ORIGINS = True` or `@cors(origins=["*"])` and no credentials, a request with no `Origin` now also gets `access-control-allow-origin: *`. The value is then the same for each request, so it needs no `Vary: Origin`. A route with `@skip_middleware("cors")` and an API with no CORS config do not change. (audit finding C1)
- **Security - each media file of a type that can run script is a download** - Bolt sent a media file as a download only when its extension was on a fixed list. The content type comes from a larger table of extensions, so some files with an HTML, XML or JavaScript type showed inline. Examples are `.xht`, `.xsd`, `.dtd`, `.es` and `.jsm`. A browser runs the script elements of each XML document. Now Bolt decides by the content type of the response. HTML, each XML type (`text/xml`, `application/xml` and each `+xml` type), each JavaScript type, WebAssembly, `text/x-component` and `application/hta` are downloads as `application/octet-stream` with `Content-Disposition: attachment`. A range request, a `HEAD` request and a `304` response get the same headers. Static files keep their own content type. The nginx example in the docs now sends each media file as a download, but a list of safe types. **Behavior change:** a media file with one of these types that showed inline, for example an XML schema (`.xsd`), is now a download. A file that was a download with its own type, for example an RSS feed, now has the type `application/octet-stream`. See [Files that can run script are downloads](docs/src/topics/media-files.md#files-that-can-run-script-are-downloads).
- **Security - JWT: a token with a `crit` header fails** - RFC 7515, section 4.1.11 makes a token invalid when the recipient does not support an extension that `crit` names. Bolt ignored `crit`, so such a token passed. Bolt supports no critical extension. Now each token with `crit` fails, whatever its value. A `"b64": false` header also fails the token, as in PyJWT. (audit finding J6)
- **Security - JWT: `null` is not a missing claim** - Bolt read `"sub": null`, `"jti": null` and a `"kid": null` header as missing, and accepted the token. PyJWT refuses each. Now a `null`, or a value of another JSON type, in `sub`, `jti`, `typ`, `exp`, `nbf`, `iat`, `kid` or `alg` fails the token. With an `issuer` or an `audience`, a `null` `iss` or `aud` fails the token, as before. (audit finding J4)
- **Security - `Requires` compares numbers by value** - A guard value of `7.0` did not equal the claim `7`, and the claim `7.0` of a private claim did not equal the guard value `7`. Thus `Requires("ver", none_of=[7.0])` let a token with `"ver": 7` pass, and `Requires("ver", 7.0)` refused it. Now a whole float in a guard is an integer, as a whole number in a token is. A guard compares a number of a private claim by value, as Python does: `7 == 7.0`. This applies to each typed and private claim, on routes, WebSocket routes and MCP tools. (audit finding J1)
- **A revocation store keeps an entry for an `exp` in the year 9999** - The store keeps an entry until `exp` plus the `leeway`. For an `exp` near the end of the year 9999, that time is after the last `datetime`. `DjangoORMRevocation` then raised `OverflowError`, and the refresh and logout routes gave a 500. Now the entry ends at the latest in the last second of the year 9999. `rotate_refresh_token()` raises `TokenRotationError` for an `exp` that a `datetime` cannot hold, before the store records the token. (audit finding J1)
- **Security - `mount()` refuses a dependency that takes a mount path parameter** - With `api.mount("/org/{org}", child)`, a dependency `def member(org: str)` of a handler of `child` read `org` from the query string. Thus `/org/acme/x?org=other` gave the dependency `"other"`, and a check of the membership used the wrong organization. With a default value, no error showed. Now `mount()` raises `ValueError` for such a dependency, as for a handler argument. This applies to sync, async and nested dependencies, and to `path("org/<org>/", api.urls)`. An argument with a marker reads the source of its marker, as on each route. Thus `Annotated[str, Path()]` reads the path, and `Annotated[str, Query()]` reads the query string. (#552)
- **A collation without case or accents cannot read a `jti` as a family or a user version** - MySQL compares text without case or accents by default. Thus `DjangoORMRevocation` found the row `version:u` for the `jti` `VERSION:u` or `vérsion:u`. A refresh of a token with such a `jti` made a row that a later logout-all wrote the user version into. That row expired with the token, the cleanup task deleted it, and the sessions that logout-all ended were live again. `revoke()` of such a `jti` gave the version row the expiry time of the token. `DjangoCacheRevocation` on a `DatabaseCache` had the same problem: `revoke("FAM:f")` ended the revocation of family `f`. Now the stores also raise `ReservedJtiError` for a `jti` that a collation can read with a reserved prefix. This includes another case, accents, a character that MySQL skips (for example a soft hyphen or a control character), a fullwidth letter, and the letter pairs i, j, y and u, v of some tailored collations. The check covers each `utf8mb4` collation of MySQL 8.0. Such a token counts as revoked and gets a 401. `bump_user_version()` now also sets the version row to never expire, so a row of an old `jti` such as `VERSION:u` cannot lose the version. A `jti` that is a UUID or a hex string is not affected. Two `jti` values that differ only in case still share one row on such a collation. A binary collation on the `jti` column keeps them apart. (#931)
- **`DjangoCacheRevocation` works on a `DatabaseCache`** - The store called the sync cache API in its async methods. A database cache runs a query for each call, and Django refuses a query on the event loop. Thus each authenticated request on a route with this store gave a 500 `SynchronousOnlyOperation`. Now the store uses the async cache API (`aget`, `aset`) for a `DatabaseCache` or a subclass. Each such call runs in a thread. Other caches stay on the sync API, so `is_revoked` on Redis, Memcached or local memory has no thread hop. `consume()` and `bump_user_version()` still raise `NotImplementedError` on a database cache. A subclass with its own `incr` now gets this error too, not `SynchronousOnlyOperation`. (#940)
- **A `(prefix, path)` entry of `STATICFILES_DIRS` is served under its prefix** - Bolt made a string of the whole tuple. Thus `runbolt` printed a wrong warning such as `cannot resolve directory ... ('vendor', PosixPath('/app/assets/vendor'))`. With `DEBUG = False`, Bolt did not serve the files of the entry. Now Bolt uses the path of the entry, and serves its files under the URL prefix `prefix/`, as the Django `FileSystemFinder` does. With `("vendor", BASE_DIR / "assets" / "vendor")`, `/static/vendor/app.css` gives `assets/vendor/app.css`, and `/static/app.css` does not. A file must stay in the directory of its entry, also through a symbolic link. A symbolic link in a static directory now has the content type of its own name, as a file from the finders has. In `MEDIA_ROOT`, a symbolic link downloads as `application/octet-stream` when its name or its target can carry script. A missing directory of such an entry gets a warning with its path only. An entry that is not a path or a `(prefix, path)` pair gets a warning, and Bolt does not use it. (#944)
- **bolt-mcp: an exposed route with a `request` argument works as a tool** - `api.mount_mcp(mcp, expose=[me])` for `async def me(request: Request)` registered the tool, but each call gave the error result `Tool 'me' failed.`. Bolt did not give the request to the handler, and the handler raised `TypeError`. A handler with `req` and other arguments also failed. Now each call of the tool gives the handler a request made from the HTTP request to the MCP endpoint. `request.headers`, `request.cookies`, `request.META` and the client address come from that request. `request.context` is the principal from the auth of the route. `request.user` and `await request.auser()` give its user, as over HTTP, or `AnonymousUser` with no user. `request.body` is empty, and `request.path` is the path of the MCP endpoint. An `@mcp.tool` with a `request` argument, and `ctx.request`, now get the same request. Before, they got an object with only `context` and `state`, and a tool argument named `req` got no request. A call of a tool with no `request` and no `Context` argument does not make a request. **Behavior change:** `mount_mcp()` now raises `ValueError` for an exposed route with a `Depends` argument, because a tool call cannot resolve a dependency. Before, each call of such a tool failed. `expose_routes()` with patterns skips such a route, as it skips a route with `File` or `Form` arguments. (#948)
- **Security - bolt-mcp: an exposed route applies its `response_model` to the tool result** - A tool call of an exposed route (`api.mount_mcp(mcp, expose=[...])`) sent the raw return value of the handler. HTTP applies the `response_model` and the return annotation, and removes the fields that the model does not have. Thus a field such as `password_hash` was not in the HTTP body, but it was in the tool result. Now Bolt serializes the return value with the response handlers of the route, as for HTTP. The tool result holds the HTTP body. This applies to a `Struct`, a `list[Struct]`, a QuerySet, a multi-response route and `validate_response=False`. A value that fails response validation gives an error result, as HTTP gives a 500. A 3xx, 4xx or 5xx response, for example `JSON(..., status_code=404)`, gives an error result with the HTTP body. A text body gives text content only. A body that is not JSON or text, for example `bytes`, gives an error result. A tool result has no headers or cookies, so Bolt does not send them. A route that validates against one `Struct` now has that schema as its `outputSchema`, unless msgspec has no JSON Schema for a field, such as an `IPv4Address`. A success body that this schema does not describe gives an error result, also the bytes of a `Response`. A text body is decoded with the charset of its media type. **Behavior change:** `mount_mcp()` raises `ValueError` for an exposed route that declares a stream or a file: a generator handler, or a return annotation or `response_class` that is a stream or file response. `expose_routes()` with patterns skips such a route. Before, each call of such a tool failed. A route that returns a stream or a file with no such declaration passes registration, and each call gives an error result. An `@mcp.tool` does not change. (#957)
- **The OpenAPI schema keeps the `msgspec.Meta` of a struct body** - For `body: Annotated[Item, msgspec.Meta(description="The item")]`, the request body schema was only `{"$ref": "#/components/schemas/Item"}`. Bolt binds such a body as `Item`, so the generator did not get the `Meta`. Now the `title`, `description`, `examples` and `extra_json_schema` keywords of the `Meta` go next to the `$ref`, as for a return annotation (#945). A constraint such as `ge` does not go in the schema, because binding ignores it on a struct. This also applies to a dataclass body, to `Annotated[Item | None, Meta(...)]`, to `Body(embed=True)` and to a body that a dependency reads. Bolt keeps the `Meta` for the schema at registration. Binding does not change: the body still decodes as `Item`. (#958)
- **The OpenAPI schema keeps the `msgspec.Meta` of a component reference** - For `-> Annotated[Item, msgspec.Meta(description="The item")]`, the response schema was only `{"$ref": "#/components/schemas/Item"}`. The generator dropped the `Meta` values. The same occurred for `list[Annotated[Item, Meta(...)]]`, for `Annotated[Item, Meta(...)] | None`, for a Struct field and for a request body item. Now the `title`, `description`, `examples` and `extra_json_schema` keywords of the `Meta` go next to the `$ref`, as OpenAPI 3.1 allows. The shared component does not change, so two uses of one component can have different descriptions. `Annotated[list[Item], Meta(...)]` and `Annotated[Item | None, Meta(...)]` already kept the `Meta`. A key of `extra_json_schema` that is not a schema keyword, such as `to_schema`, gave a 500 from `/docs/openapi.json`. Now the generator ignores it, as it ignores other unknown keys. (#945)
- **Security - bolt-mcp: an exposed route checks token revocation** - #939 ran the auth and the guards of an exposed route on each tool call, but not the revocation check of the route. Thus a revoked JWT got a 401 over HTTP, but the handler ran for the same token over MCP. Now each call of the tool runs the `revoked_token_handler` of the route, as HTTP does. This also applies to a handler from `revocation_store`, and to a handler that checks the family or the user version from the claims. A revoked token gets `Permission denied for tool '<name>'`, and the handler and tool middleware do not run. A jti that the store refuses counts as revoked, as over HTTP. An error of the store gives an error result, and Bolt logs the traceback. `tools/list` does not check revocation, because the check needs a store lookup for each tool. A route with no revocation pays nothing. (#951)
- **A nested Struct of a reverse relation with a `related_query_name`, and a reverse one-to-one with no row** - Name the Struct field of a reverse relation as the attribute of the instance: the `related_name`. A query uses the `related_query_name`. When the two names were different, a `.values()` QuerySet response gave a 500 `FieldError`. A page of `@paginate`, a `ViewSet` retrieve and an async handler did not load the relation, so a field with a default got `null` and not the related row. Now Bolt uses the query name in `.values()` and `select_related()`, and the attribute name to load and read instances. A field with the query name also works now on each path. A reverse one-to-one with no row and a field `X | None` with no default gave a 500 `Object missing required field` for a page of `@paginate`, a `ViewSet` retrieve, a model instance and a list of model instances. Now the field is `null`, as with a `.values()` QuerySet. msgspec reads such an instance through a view that gives `None` for the relation with no row. Each other field comes from the instance, as before. Only a Struct that nests a reverse one-to-one relation takes this path. A hidden reverse relation (`related_name="+"`) has no attribute on the instance. The `related_query_name` of a `GenericRelation` gives a manager. A `.values()` QuerySet response with no pagination still reads the two. The paths that read model instances, a page included, gave a 500 with an error from Django or msgspec. Now they give a `TypeError` that names the field. (#949)
- **`TestClient(api, follow_redirects=False)` works** - The constructor of `TestClient` and `AsyncTestClient` gave `follow_redirects=True` to httpx, and the value of the caller too. Thus `TestClient(api, follow_redirects=False)` raised `TypeError`. Now `follow_redirects` is a parameter of the two clients. The default stays `True`. (#915)
- **`TestClient` serves the OpenAPI docs routes** - `runbolt` registers `/docs`, `/docs/openapi.json` and the UI routes, but `TestClient` and `AsyncTestClient` did not. Thus a test got a 404 from them. Now the clients register the docs routes with the code of `runbolt`. The `auth`, `guards` and `enabled` values of the `OpenAPIConfig` apply. The clients do not add the docs routes to the API, so a later `mount()` of the API does not copy them. **Behavior change:** a route of the API with the path of a docs route now raises `ValueError` in the client, as `runbolt` refuses to start with it. To keep such a route, set `enabled=False` on the `OpenAPIConfig`, or set another `path`. The first client of an API takes about 4 ms more to register the docs routes. Before, clients of one API that started in parallel threads could raise `RuntimeError: dictionary changed size during iteration`, also without docs routes. Now each client makes its changes to the API before the next client reads the API. (#932)
- **`request.user` and `await request.auser()` give the same user when there is no user** - On a route with no Django `AuthenticationMiddleware`, `request.user` was `None` and `await request.auser()` gave `AnonymousUser`. This occurred on a route with no auth, on a route with `JWTAuthentication` and no token, and with `APIKeyAuthentication`. Now both give the same `AnonymousUser`, as Django's `AuthenticationMiddleware` does. Bolt makes it on the first read only, so a route that does not read the user pays nothing. Routes with `SessionAuthentication` or `DjangoMiddlewareStack` already gave `AnonymousUser`, and do not change. Without `django.contrib.auth` in `INSTALLED_APPS`, both give `None`. Before, `await request.auser()` raised `RuntimeError` there. **Behavior change:** `request.user is None` is now false with no user, and `AnonymousUser` is true in a boolean test. Replace `if request.user:` with `if request.user.is_authenticated:`. Replace `if not request.user:` and `if request.user is None:` with `if not request.user.is_authenticated:`. Without `django.contrib.auth`, `request.user` stays `None`, so check `request.user is not None` first in such a project. A valid token for a deleted or inactive user does not change. (#916)
- **A model instance with a nested Struct of a relation** - #913 loads a nested Struct of a foreign key or a one-to-one relation for a QuerySet. Other paths read model instances, and they still failed. A page of `@paginate`, a `ViewSet` retrieve with a plain Struct `serializer_class`, and a handler that returns a model instance gave a 500 `Object missing required field`. The relation did not load on the event loop. A field with a default got the default and not the related row. With `validate_response=False`, a list of model instances gave the related instance to the JSON encoder, and the response was a 500. Now Bolt loads the relations that the Struct names before it reads the instances. A page QuerySet gets `select_related()` with these relations. Other instances get one query for each relation that is not loaded. With `validate_response=False`, a nested Struct field gives an object. A nested Struct of a reverse foreign key or a many-to-many relation gave a 500 `Expected array`, and the parent rows repeated. Now it gives a `TypeError` that names the field. Use a Bolt `Serializer` for such a response. (#929)
- **bolt-mcp: an exposed route keeps its auth and its guards** - `api.mount_mcp(mcp, expose=[handler])` ran the route with the auth of the mount only. The `auth` and the guards of the route did not run. Thus an anonymous MCP call got the result of a route that gives 401 over HTTP. Now each call of the tool runs the auth and the guards of the route in Rust, on the headers of the MCP request. This includes the defaults from `BOLT_AUTHENTICATION_CLASSES` and `BOLT_DEFAULT_PERMISSION_CLASSES`, and the cookie CSRF check. The auth and the guards of the mount still run first. A refused call gets the error result `Permission denied for tool '<name>'`, as for the guards of an `@mcp.tool`, and the handler does not run. `tools/list` does not show the tool to a caller that the route refuses. Tool middleware gets the principal from the auth of the route. **Behavior change:** a tool call cannot run middleware. Thus `mount_mcp()` now raises `ValueError` for an exposed route with `@rate_limit`, Python middleware or Django middleware, also when the middleware is on the API or the router. The error names the route and the middleware. Write an `@mcp.tool(guards=...)` for such an operation. (#928)
- **A response class in `Annotated` or in a `type` alias** - `-> Annotated[JSON, ...]`, `-> Annotated[Item | JSON, ...]` and `type Body = Item | JSON` gave a 500 `Response validation error`. Bolt did not find the response class in `Annotated` or in an alias, so it validated the body against `JSON`. Now these annotations act as `-> JSON` and `-> Item | JSON` do. A `None` result of `-> Annotated[Item | None, ...]`, or of an alias of `Item | None`, gave a 500 when the route did not validate. Now it is the JSON `null`. The OpenAPI schema uses the same rule. Before, `-> JSON` and `-> Response` had the schema `{"type": "object"}`, which refuses a list. Now `-> JSON` has an empty `application/json` schema, and `-> Response` has an empty `*/*` schema. Thus each body is valid. `-> Item | JSON` documents the schema of `Item`, and `-> Annotated[Item | JSON, ...]` documents the schema of `-> Annotated[Item, ...]`. A class with a media type in a union, such as `HTML` in `-> Item | HTML`, adds its media type. `-> str | JSON` documents a text/plain string and an application/json string. `-> Annotated[str, ...]` documents text/plain, as `-> str` does. (#930)
- **With `DEBUG`, a static file from a finder stays in its directory** - Bolt checks that a file in `STATIC_ROOT` or `STATICFILES_DIRS` does not resolve, through a symbolic link, to a path out of its directory. With `DEBUG = True`, Bolt also serves the files that the Django staticfiles finders find, for example from the `static/` directory of an app or from a `(prefix, path)` entry of `STATICFILES_DIRS`. These files did not get the check, so a symbolic link in such a directory could serve each file that the server can read. Now the canonical path of a found file must be in the canonical directory of the finder location that found it. Else the response is `404`, as for `STATIC_ROOT`. Bolt reads and resolves the directories of the finders one time, at startup. A request resolves only a directory that did not exist at startup. A finder with no filesystem storage has no known directory, so Bolt does not serve its files and prints a warning at startup. (#933)
- **A revocation store refuses a key that it cannot keep** - `RevokedToken.jti` has 255 characters. A longer `jti` gave a 500 with `DataError` on PostgreSQL, and a `jti` with a NUL character gave a 500 on each request. MySQL without strict mode can cut a long `jti` short, so two tokens could share one row. A memcached backend raises `InvalidCacheKey` for a key with a space or a key longer than 250 characters, and the request got a 500. Now `DjangoORMRevocation` raises `ReservedJtiError` for a key longer than the `max_length` of the `jti` field of its model, or for a key with a NUL character. `DjangoCacheRevocation` raises `ReservedJtiError` when the cache backend refuses the key. The checks apply to the `jti`, and to the keys that the stores make from `fam` and `sub`. Such a token counts as revoked: authentication gives 401, and `/auth/refresh`, `/auth/logout` and `/auth/logout-all` give 401. `rotate_refresh_token` raises `TokenRotationError`. A `jti` from `create_token_pair` is a UUID, so Bolt tokens are not affected. (#931)
- **A QuerySet response with a nested Struct of a relation** - A handler with the return type `list[Item]` can return a QuerySet. Bolt loaded it with `.values()` and the field names of `Item`. A field of `Item` with a Struct type for a foreign key got the key, not an object, and the response was a 500. `select_related()` did not help. Now Bolt reads the fields of the nested Struct across the relation, in the same query. A foreign key and a one-to-one relation work, also when a nested Struct has a nested Struct. A relation with no object gives `null`. A Struct that nests itself across a relation gives a clear `TypeError`. Bolt makes this plan once for each model. A Struct with no nested Struct keeps the plain `.values()` path. (#913)
- **A `-> JSON` or `-> Response` return annotation sends the body** - Bolt validated the body of the response against the class in the annotation. Thus `JSON({"a": 1})` from a handler with `-> JSON` gave a 500 `Response validation error`, also with a `status_code` such as 201 on the route. A union with a response class, such as `-> Item | JSON`, gave a 500 for each `JSON` or dict result, because msgspec cannot validate such a union. Now a response class in a return annotation names the class of the result, not the type of the body. `-> JSON`, `-> JSON | None` and `-> JSON | Redirect` send the body as it is. `-> Item | JSON` validates the body against `Item`, as `-> Item` does. Use `response_model=` to validate the body of a `-> JSON` route and to document its schema. (#912)
- **A `jti` cannot change a family or a user version in a revocation store** - `DjangoCacheRevocation` keeps a token at `revoked:<jti>`, a family at `revoked:fam:<fam>` and a user version at `revoked:ver:<user_id>`. Thus `revoke("ver:u")` set the version of user `u` to 1, and `/auth/logout-all` gave a 500 on Redis. `DjangoORMRevocation` had the same problem with `family:` and `version:`. Now `revoke`, `consume` and `is_revoked` raise `ReservedJtiError`, a subclass of `ValueError`, for a `jti` with one of these prefixes. The key names do not change, so each revoked token with a UUID `jti` stays revoked after the upgrade. A token whose `jti` has a reserved prefix counts as revoked: authentication gives 401, and the WebSocket handshake fails. `/auth/logout` and `/auth/logout-all` give 401, and `rotate_refresh_token` raises `TokenRotationError`. A `jti` from `create_token_pair` is a UUID, so Bolt tokens are not affected. (#887)
- **`consume` refuses a token after `exp + leeway`** - `consume` of such a token returned `True` on each call, because the cache deleted the entry at once. Now `DjangoCacheRevocation` and `DjangoORMRevocation` return `False` for such a token and write no entry. `JWTAuthentication` accepts a token to the end of the second `exp + leeway`. Thus each entry of the two stores now also lives to the end of that second. Before, an entry for a token with `exp + leeway` equal to now expired at once. (#887)
- **`runbolt --processes 2` and more writes the log records of the workers** - Without a `LOGGING` setting, Bolt sends log records through a queue. One thread reads the queue and writes each record. This thread ran only in the supervisor process, because a fork does not copy threads. Thus each record of a worker stayed in its queue, and warnings and tracebacks from handlers did not show. Now each worker starts its own thread after the fork. Before it exits, the worker also writes the records that are still in the queue. (#909)
- **`ModelViewSet` create and update take the primary key of a foreign key field** - A serializer field for a ForeignKey or OneToOneField can hold the primary key of the related row, for example `user: int`. `create`, `update` and `partial_update` gave this value to the relation, and Django raised `ValueError`. The client got a 500. Now the value goes to the column attribute (`user_id`), as in `Serializer.to_model()`. A model instance and the dict of a nested serializer stay on the relation. `update` and `partial_update` do not write the lookup field through its relation. (#914)
- **The middleware and the access log get the 404, 405, 401 and 403 that Rust gives before the handler** - Rust answers a path with no route (404), a method that the path does not have (405), and a rejection by authentication or a guard (401, 403). Django runs each middleware for such a request. No Python middleware and no Django middleware ran for these requests. Thus a request-id, tenant or security-header middleware did not see them. Also, the 404 had no access-log line. Now the Python middleware and the Django middleware run for these requests, and get the response of Rust as the response of the handler. The handler does not run, and `process_view` does not run. `request.body` is empty. A path with no route runs the middleware of the API. A rejection runs the middleware of its route. The 404 has an access-log line, with the `skip_paths`, `skip_status_codes` and `sample_rate` of the API. With Python middleware or the access log, each 404 takes the GIL. A 429 from a rate limit stays in Rust: the middleware does not get it. An API with no Python middleware answers in Rust, as before, with no middleware call. A query value over the length limit does not give a 422 for a 404 or a 405. See [Responses from Rust](docs/src/topics/middleware.md#responses-from-rust). (#865)
- **SIGHUP and SIGQUIT stop the workers of `runbolt --processes 2` and more** - The supervisor process had no handler for these signals. Thus a signal stopped the supervisor, and its workers continued to serve on the port with no parent. A terminal that closes, or an `ssh` session that ends, sends SIGHUP. A later `runbolt` on the same port then shared the port with these workers, because of `SO_REUSEPORT`. Now the supervisor stops its workers gracefully on SIGHUP and SIGQUIT, as on SIGTERM, and then exits with status 0. (#910)
- **`TestClient(raise_server_exceptions=True)` raises the exception of a handler** - The option had an effect only on errors of the test backend. A handler that raised gave the test a 500 and no exception. Now the client raises the exception of a server error in the test, as Starlette's test client does. This applies to sync and async handlers, to routes with Django middleware, and to the body of a streaming response. `AsyncTestClient` does the same. The client does not raise an exception that the API answers with a response, for example an `HTTPException` or the 4xx of a validation error. **Behavior change:** the option is `True` by default, so a test that expects the 500 of a handler exception now gets the exception. Give such a test `raise_server_exceptions=False`. `runbolt` does not change. (#869)
- **The OpenAPI operation of a paginated route lists the pagination parameters** - A route with `@paginate`, or the list action of a viewset with `pagination_class`, reads query parameters for the page. Its operation did not list them, so Swagger UI and generated clients could not send them. Now `PageNumberPagination` documents `page` and `page_size`, `LimitOffsetPagination` documents `limit` and `offset`, and `CursorPagination` documents `cursor` and `page_size`. The name of the page size parameter comes from `page_size_query_param`. When it is `None`, the operation has no page size parameter. The schema of the page size has the `page_size` default and the `max_page_size` maximum of the class. A query parameter of the handler with the same name stays as it is. (#917)
- **`/ready` gives status 503 when a check fails** - `register_health_checks()` adds `/ready`. A load balancer or a Kubernetes readiness probe reads only the status code. `/ready` gave status 200 with `"status": "unhealthy"` in the body, so the probe saw a process with a failed check as ready. **Behavior change:** now `/ready` gives status 503 with the same body when a check fails or raises. The global list of checks also had the database check only by chance. After `add_health_check()`, the database check was not in the list. If `/ready` ran first, the database check stayed. Now the database check is in the list from import time, and `add_health_check()` adds a check after it. (#911)
- **`logger_name` of `LoggingConfig` has an effect on the access log** - The docs showed `LoggingConfig(logger_name="myapp.api")`, but the Rust access log of `runbolt` always wrote to `django.server`. Only `log_exception()` used the name. Now the access log writes the line of each route to the logger of its API: `BoltAPI(logging_config=LoggingConfig(logger_name="myapp.api"))`. Each API keeps its own logger after `mount()`. The 308 redirect uses the logger of the route at the other slash form. The automatic `OPTIONS` answer uses the logger of the route for the first method in its `Allow` header. Bolt reads each logger and its level once, at startup, and Rust keeps them. The request path does not look up a logger. Each worker thread writes one 429 line to each logger for each second. The access log is on when a route writes to a logger at `ERROR` or a lower level. `BoltAPI()` raises `ValueError` for a name that is empty, `None` or not a str. **Behavior change:** the lines of an API with a `logger_name` leave `django.server`. In `LOGGING`, give that logger the level and the handlers of `django.server`, or remove `logger_name`. (#866)
- **bolt-mcp: a dict tool result with an iterator has the same values in its text and in `structuredContent`** - A dict that held a `map`, a `zip` or a generator had the values in its text and an empty array in `structuredContent`. The text encode used up the iterator. Now Bolt converts the result one time and uses it for both outputs. (#875)
- **A Django response from a handler sends its cookies** - A handler can return a Django `HttpResponse` with a cookie from `set_cookie()`. Bolt did not send the cookie. Now Bolt sends it, as for each other response type.
- **A sync read on an event loop can start a loop of its own on Python 3.14** - `run_orm_blocking` runs a sync read, such as `request.user`, on the thread of the event loop. It hides the running loop for that time. Python 3.14 keeps the current task for each thread, and the read did not hide it. Thus a loop that the read started on the same thread, for example with `asyncio.run()`, could not start its task and waited with no end. Now the read also hides the current task and puts it back after the read.
- **A `WebSocket` has one lifecycle state, and a wrong call names the fix** - `WebSocket` kept a `state` and a separate `_accepted` flag. The two could disagree. Thus `accept()` after `close()` before accept sent a second handshake decision and set the state back to `CONNECTED`. Now `state` is the only field, and it only moves forward: `CONNECTING`, `CONNECTED`, `DISCONNECTED`. `accept()` after close raises `RuntimeError("WebSocket is closed: accept() cannot open it again")`. Before, it did nothing after an accept. `send()` before `accept()` raises `RuntimeError("WebSocket is not accepted: call accept() before send()")`, and `send()` after close says that the WebSocket is closed. Before, both said `WebSocket is not connected`. `receive_text()`, `receive_bytes()` and `receive_json()` before `accept()` raise `RuntimeError`. Before, `receive_text()` gave `""` from the `websocket.connect` event, and the next call waited with no end. `receive()` before `accept()` still gives the `websocket.connect` event. A second `accept()` or `close()` still does nothing.
- **`WebSocketTestClient` iterators give the messages that the server sent before it closed** - `iter_text()`, `iter_bytes()` and `iter_json()` stopped when the handler had closed the connection. They did not give the messages that were still in the queue. A handler that sent its messages and closed gave an empty iteration. Now the iterators give each message and stop at the close. A `receive` on a closed connection with no more messages raises `ConnectionClosed` at once. Before, it waited for the timeout.
- **`CursorPagination` does not skip rows with equal ordering values** - The cursor held only the ordering value of the last row. The next page started after that value. When a page ended inside a group of rows with the same value, the rest of the group did not show. For example, with `ordering = "-created_at"`, rows from the same instant could be lost. Now Bolt also orders by the primary key, and the cursor holds the value and the primary key. An ordering by the primary key keeps the old cursor. A cursor from an earlier version still works, but it can skip rows in its group. A `values()` queryset must include the primary key. Without it, the page raises `ValueError`.
- **A dead refresh token cannot log out live sessions** - The `/auth/logout` and `/auth/logout-all` routes of `JWTAuthViews` did not check the refresh token in the store. After a refresh, the old token could revoke the family of the new token. After a logout-all, an old token could log out each later login again. Now the two routes reject a used, revoked or old-version refresh token with a 401, as `/auth/refresh` does.
- **`exclude_unset=True` leaves out the fields that hold `msgspec.UNSET`** - `Serializer.dump`, `dump_json`, `dump_many`, `dump_many_json`, `to_dict` and the same methods of a view accepted `exclude_unset`, but did not read it. The result always had each field. Now `exclude_unset=True` leaves out each field whose value is `msgspec.UNSET`. Give an optional field the default `msgspec.UNSET` to tell an absent field from a `null`, for example in a `PATCH` body.
- **A bad `File()` limit raises at once** - Rust did not read a `max_size`, `min_size` or `max_files` of the wrong type. The route got no limit, with no error. For example, `File(max_size="10MB")` allowed a file of any size up to `BOLT_MAX_UPLOAD_SIZE`. Now `File()` raises `TypeError` for a limit that is not an int, and a `bool` is not an int here. It raises `ValueError` for a `max_size` or `min_size` below 0, a `max_files` below 1, a limit above `sys.maxsize`, or a `min_size` above `max_size`. Rust also ignored an int too large for it. The error names the limit and shows the value. `None` still means no limit.
- **`Router.include_router(child, prefix=...)` applies the prefix to each route of the child** - A router stored the full path of a route when the route was added. Thus the `prefix` of `include_router` applied only to the routes that the child got after the call. A route from before the call kept its old path. Now a router adds its prefix when Bolt reads its routes, so each route of the child has the prefix.
- **`Token.create(is_staff=True)` no longer makes an admin token** - `Token.create` set `is_admin` for each staff user, but its comment said that an admin implies staff. Thus `Requires("is_admin")` allowed each token from `Token.create(is_staff=True)`. Now an admin implies staff: `is_admin=True` also sets `is_staff`, and `is_staff=True` alone keeps `is_admin` false. `create_jwt_for_user` does not use `Token.create`, so its tokens do not change.
- **`Router` does not accept `dependencies`** - `Router(dependencies=[...])` stored the list, and no code read it. The dependencies did not run, with no error. A check in such a dependency, for example for authentication, protected no route. Now the argument raises `TypeError`. Use `guards` and `auth` on the router, or `Depends` in the handler signature.
- **`str()` of an `HTTPException` shows its detail once** - `str(NotFound())` gave `404: Not Found Not Found`. The status text was in `args`, and `__str__` added the detail again. This text goes to logs and tracebacks. Now `str()` of an `HTTPException` gives `<status>: <detail>`, for example `404: Not Found`.
- **A closed `TestClient` does not run the lifespan again** - A second `with client:` on a closed `TestClient` ran the startup and the shutdown of the lifespan again. Then httpx raised `Cannot reopen a client instance`. Now the client raises `RuntimeError("TestClient is closed. ...")` before the startup. A client entered without `with`, by `__enter__()`, did not run the lifespan shutdown on `close()`, and its event loop stayed open. Now `close()` runs the shutdown once. It also stops sharing the database connection of the test, so a later client on another thread does not raise `SharedTestConnectionError`. A second `with client:` on an open client raises `RuntimeError` before a second startup, and a lifespan shutdown that raises still closes the client. `AsyncTestClient` and `aclose()` do the same.
- **`rotate_refresh_token` rejects claims that are not from a refresh token** - The function did not read the `typ` claim. It relied on `JWTAuthentication(token_type="refresh")` on the route. On a route without `token_type="refresh"`, `rotate_refresh_token(claims, rotate=False)` gave a new access token for the claims of an access token. Thus an access token could renew itself with no end. Now the function raises `TokenRotationError` when `typ` is not `refresh`, in both modes. Claims with no `typ` claim also get this error.
- **`request.read()` in a Django middleware gives `b""` for a request with no body** - The bridge made the stream of the Django request only for a request with a body. Thus `request.read()` in a middleware raised `AttributeError` for a `GET` with no body, and the client got a 500. `request.body` already gave `b""`. Now the stream of an empty body is empty, as in Django.
- **A token subject that is too large for the database gives no user** - A numeric subject above 64 bits, such as a 21-digit OIDC `sub`, made `load_user_by_pk_sync` raise `OverflowError` on SQLite. The default user loader raised that error, and `JWTAuthentication` printed it to stderr. Now the loader returns `None`, as it does for a primary key that no user has.
- **A JSON decode error gives its column in characters** - Bolt took the column of a JSON syntax error from the byte position of the error. A body with a multi-byte UTF-8 character before the error on the same line gave a column that was too large, and the `^` marker was too far right. Now the line number and the column count characters. `ctx["byte_position"]` still holds the byte position.
- **`Token.decode` does no audience check for an empty audience** - `Token.decode(token, secret, audience="")` raised `ValueError` (`Invalid audience`) for a token with an `aud` claim. `audience=None` accepted the same token, and `issuer=""` did no issuer check. Now an empty `audience` asks for no check, as `None` and an empty `issuer` do.
- **`request.user` has the field types of the ORM** - Bolt loads `request.user` with a SELECT that it compiles once. It built the user from the raw database row, without the converters that a QuerySet applies. On SQLite, a `UUIDField` primary key was a hex `str`, and with `USE_TZ = True`, `date_joined` and `last_login` were naive datetimes. Other converted fields had the same problem. Now Bolt finds the converters with the SELECT, once, and applies them to each row. The user has the same values as `User.objects.get(pk=...)`.
- **`WebSocketTestClient` refuses the messages that the server refuses** - The test client sent each ASGI message of a handler or a mounted app to the client. The server refuses some of them. Now the test client raises the same errors as the server. It raises `RuntimeError` for a `websocket.send` before accept and for a second handshake decision. It raises `ConnectionResetError` for a `websocket.send` after a refused handshake. It raises `ValueError` for a `websocket.send` with no `text` or `bytes` and for an unknown message type. It raises `KeyError` for a message with no `type`, and `TypeError` for a `text` that is not a str or `bytes` that are not bytes. Before, a test could pass where the server raised.
- **`@paginate` accepts a sync handler** - The wrapper of `@paginate` awaited the result of each handler. A sync handler returns a QuerySet, not a coroutine. Thus each request gave a 500 response with `TypeError: object QuerySet can't be used in 'await' expression`. Now `@paginate` finds at decoration whether the handler is a coroutine function, and awaits only an async handler. A sync handler must return a lazy QuerySet or a list, because Bolt calls it on the event loop. The pagination runs the queries on the ORM executor, as for an async handler.
- **A bad `@rate_limit` value stops startup** - Rust did not read an `rps`, `burst` or `key` of the wrong type. The route got the default limit of 100 rps with burst 200, with no error. For example, `@rate_limit(rps=0.5)` allowed 100 requests per second. Now `@rate_limit` raises `TypeError` for an `rps` or `burst` that is not an int, or a `key` that is not a str. It raises `ValueError` for an `rps` or `burst` less than 1, or an empty `key`. A `rate_limit` dict in the `middleware` of an API or a router gets the same check in Rust at startup. A bad value raises `TypeError`. Before, `burst=0` gave twice the rps.
- **A `CursorPagination` cursor is safe in a query string, and a cursor with a wrong value gives the first page** - The cursor used the standard base64 alphabet, which has `+` and `/`. A `+` in a query string is a space. Thus a `next_cursor` that the client sent back as it was did not decode. The client then got the first page again with the same cursor, without end. Now the cursor uses the URL-safe alphabet. A cursor in the standard alphabet from an earlier release still works. A cursor with a value of the wrong type for the ordering field or the primary key gave a 500. An example is `{"v":"x"}` with `ordering = "-id"`. Now such a cursor gives the first page, as a cursor that is not base64 does.
- **`runbolt --host localhost` binds only the loopback address** - The server read `--host` as an IP address. For each other value, it bound `0.0.0.0` with no error. Thus `--host localhost` answered on all interfaces. Now the server resolves a host name and binds its first IPv4 address, as Django's `runserver` does. A name with no IPv4 address binds its first IPv6 address. A host name that does not resolve stops startup with an error that names the host. An IPv6 address in brackets, such as `[::]`, now binds that IPv6 address. Before, it bound `0.0.0.0`. Brackets hold only an IPv6 address, so `[localhost]` stops startup. A property test and the `bind_addr` fuzz target check these rules with a fake resolver.
- **A page number or an offset beyond the data is not a server error** - `PageNumberPagination` did not limit the page number when the result had no rows. `?page=3` gave `page: 3` with `has_previous: true`. A very large page number gave a 500 on SQLite, because the row offset did not fit the database integer. `LimitOffsetPagination` sent each offset to the database, so `?offset=9223372036854775808` gave the same 500. Now a result with no rows gives page 1 for each page number. An offset at or after the last row gives no items, and Bolt runs no query for them. The page-number example in the pagination docs now shows the keys that the response has.
- **`BOLT_ALLOWED_FILE_PATHS` works on Windows and macOS** - On Windows, Rust `canonicalize` gives a path such as `\\?\C:\srv`. Python gives `C:\srv` for the same allowed directory. The two did not match, so each `FileResponse` gave a 403. Now Bolt compares the paths without the `\\?\` part. It also uses the real name of each directory, so an 8.3 short name or another case matches. On macOS, `realpath` accepts `..` after a file. A client could thus learn whether a file existed out of the allowed directories. Now Bolt walks a path with `..` as on Linux. macOS allows 32 links in one lookup, so Bolt stops there too. Platform smoke tests cover these cases on macOS and Windows.
- **A str `BOLT_ALLOWED_FILE_PATHS` stops startup** - Bolt read each character of a str as an allowed directory. Thus `"/"` was an allowed directory, and `FileResponse` served each file on the server. Now a value that is not a list or a tuple of absolute paths stops startup with `ImproperlyConfigured`. A set, a generator or a dict also stops startup now. A path can be a str or a `Path`. A relative path, such as `""` or `Path("")`, depends on the working directory, so it stops startup. So do `None` and bytes in the list.
- **`JWTAuthViews` works with an asymmetric algorithm: `public_key=`** - The views had one `secret` for the signature and for the verification. With `algorithm="RS256"` and a private key, `/auth/login` gave the cookies. Then `/auth/refresh`, `/auth/logout` and `/auth/logout-all` gave a 401 for each request, because the refresh routes used the private key as the verification key. Now `JWTAuthViews` has the argument `public_key`. The views sign with `secret`, the PEM private key, and verify the refresh token with `public_key`. An asymmetric algorithm with no `secret` or no `public_key` raises `ImproperlyConfigured` at startup. So does a `public_key` with an `HS*` algorithm.
- **An error in a streaming body is logged** - When the body of a `StreamingResponse` raised an error, the body ended with no log. The client got the status of the response with a short body, and nothing showed the cause. Now Bolt logs the error with its traceback to the `django_bolt.streaming` logger, at level ERROR. The status is already sent, so the body still ends there. This also applies to an error in `__iter__`. A sync iterator that is not a generator could go on after an error, and Bolt called it again. Now the first error ends the body. An async iterable whose `__aiter__` raised made the response wait with no end. Now that error also ends the body and is logged. An error of `aclose()` in the cleanup is logged as a WARNING.
- **A repeated form field that is not a sequence keeps its last value** - A form field with a type such as `str` or `int` got a `list` when the client sent its name two times. Thus a handler that used the value as its type raised an error, and any client could cause a 500. Now the handler gets the last value, as for a repeated query key. A `list`, `set`, `frozenset` or `tuple` field still takes each value, and an `Any` field still gets the list.
- **A path out of `BOLT_ALLOWED_FILE_PATHS` is a 403, also when it names no file** - A `FileResponse` path out of the allowed directories gave a 403 for a file, but not for a missing path or a directory. When the path came from the request, a client could learn which files exist out of the allowed directories. Now Bolt follows the path part by part, with its links, as `realpath` does. Where the lookup stops, Bolt completes the path as text. If that path is out of the allowed directories, Bolt answers 403. If it is in them, a path that names no file still gives a 404, also when an allowed directory does not exist yet. A path through a link out of the allowed directories also gives a 403. A directory on the way to an allowed directory that the server cannot search gives a logged 500.
- **Two body parameters each get their own type** - A handler with two body parameters of different types, and no dependency, decoded the body one time, as the type of the first parameter. The second parameter got that object, and Bolt did not validate the body against the second type. Now Bolt decodes the body one time for each type, as a handler with a dependency does. Parameters of one type still share one decode.
- **Django's `Http404` gives a 404** - A handler that raised `Http404` got a 500 with two ERROR records. This included `get_object_or_404` for a missing object, so any client could cause it. Now Bolt answers 404, as Django does, and it does not log the error as a server error. With `DEBUG=False`, the detail is `Not Found`, because the message can name the model. With `DEBUG=True`, the detail is the message. An `error_formatter` gets the kind `not_found` and the detail `Not Found`.
- **A model instance or other object converts to a response Struct by attribute** - A handler can return an object, such as a Django model instance, for a `Struct` or `list[Struct]` response type. Bolt read each declared field with `getattr(obj, name, None)` and validated the dict. Thus a missing attribute became `None` and replaced the field default, and a renamed field (`rename="camel"` or `msgspec.field(name=...)`) failed with "missing required field". Both gave a 500. Now msgspec reads the object by attribute (`from_attributes=True`), so defaults and renamed fields work. An object for an `Item | None` response type now converts too.
- **A request with no body gives the default of an optional body parameter** - A body parameter with a default, such as `user: User | None = None`, gave a 422 (`Input data was truncated`) for a request with no body. The default had no effect. Now a request with no body gives the default, or `None` for an `Optional` type without default. A required body parameter still gives a 422, and a body that is present must be valid JSON of the type.
- **A QuerySet for a renamed `list[Struct]` response works** - Bolt loads a QuerySet with `.values()` of the declared fields. The rows had the Python field names, so a Struct with `rename=` or `msgspec.field(name=...)` failed validation with a 500. Without validation, the JSON had the Python names. Now each row has the wire names of the Struct. This also applies to model instances that Bolt projects when `validate_response=False`.
- **A sequence field of a header model takes the header value** - A `Header()` model with a `list`, `set`, `frozenset` or `tuple` field gave a 422 (`Expected array, got str`) for each request that had the header. Now the field gets a sequence of one item, as a sequence header parameter and a cookie model do. The item converts to its type, so `list[int]` works. Bolt does not split the value at a comma.
- **A mounted ASGI app cannot send the body before the start** - ASGI requires `http.response.start` before `http.response.body`. Bolt kept a body chunk that came first and sent it after the start. Thus an app that broke the order got a different body than under uvicorn, which raises. Now `send()` raises `RuntimeError("http.response.body sent before http.response.start")`.
- **A struct parameter error names its source as a scalar parameter error does** - A 422 error for a field of a struct parameter had `"query parameter"` or `"form field"` as the first item of `loc`. A scalar parameter has `"query"` or `"body"` there. Now a struct with `Query()` gives `["query", ...]`, and a struct with `Form()` gives `["body", ...]` for a missing field too. `Cookie()` keeps `["cookie", ...]`. A missing field with `msgspec.field(name=...)` now gives its wire name in `loc` and `msg`, as an invalid field does.
- **`Serializer.subset()` keeps each validator of a field** - `subset()` copied the validators of a field to the new class with one name for each field. Thus the subset kept only the last `@field_validator` of a field, and ran no other validator of that field. Data that the parent serializer rejects could pass the subset. Now the subset has all the validators of its fields, in the order of the parent.
- **`WebSocketTestClient` uses the Origin check of `runbolt`** - The test client had its own copy of the WebSocket Origin check, and the copy accepted an `Origin` value that is not ASCII. Now `WebSocketTestClient` and `runbolt` use the same check. For an `Origin` value that is not ASCII, the test client raises `PermissionError`, and `runbolt` answers HTTP 403.
- **`dump()` of a serializer with `rename=` or `tag=` keeps the Python values** - For a serializer with `rename=` or a tag, `dump()` and `dump_many()` gave a `datetime`, `UUID` or `Decimal` as a string and a set as a list. With an option such as `exclude_none=True`, through a view, or for a serializer with no rename, the same data gave the Python values. Now `dump()` changes only the keys on each path. The JSON of a response does not change.
- **A route-level `@cors` allows only its own origins** - A route with `@cors(origins=[...])` did not use `CORS_ALLOWED_ORIGINS`, but it still allowed each origin that matched `CORS_ALLOWED_ORIGIN_REGEXES`. With `credentials=True`, such an origin got credentialed CORS on a route that did not list it. Now the origins of a route are its complete allow list. A route that used the global patterns must list them in `origin_regexes`. The most specific `cors` config now applies: the route over the router, and the router over the API. Before, the first `cors` config applied, so an API config hid the config of a route. A config with no origins and no patterns keeps the allow list of the outer config.
- **`Serializer.model_validate()` raises `RequestValidationError` for a validator error** - When a `@field_validator` or `@model_validator` rejected a dict, `model_validate()` raised `msgspec.ValidationError` with all the messages in one string. `model_validate_json()` raised `RequestValidationError` for the same data, as the documentation says. Now `model_validate()` also raises `RequestValidationError`, with one entry for each error in `errors()`.
- **A path with repeated slashes keeps the CORS config of its route** - The CORS middleware read the path before Bolt merged its repeated slashes. For `/api//admin`, it found no route and used the global CORS config. Thus an origin in `CORS_ALLOWED_ORIGINS` could read a route whose `@cors` origins did not list it. Now the middleware reads the merged path, which the router uses.
- **The pattern types of `django_bolt.serializers` reject a value with a line break at its end** - Python reads `$` in a pattern as the end of the string or the position before a last line break. Thus `Email`, `URL`, `HttpsURL`, `Slug`, `UUID`, `IPv4`, `IPv6`, `IP`, `Username`, `Phone`, `HexColor`, `Timezone` and the other pattern types accepted a value such as `"a@b.co\n"`, and the handler got the line break. Now each pattern ends with `$(?!\n)`, and such a value gives a 422.
- **`runbolt --dev` exits when its file watcher stops** - When the file watcher stopped, the supervisor did not see it as an end. With a live server, it logged `watcher error: File watcher disconnected` every 200 ms. After the server exited, it waited with no log, and no change could reload it. Now the supervisor stops the server and exits with code 1, with the message `file watcher stopped`. A watcher error from notify is now logged also while the server is down. Before, the supervisor did not show it then. The same error from consecutive scans is logged once, so an unreadable directory does not repeat its error on each poll.
- **Each OpenAPI operation has its own `operationId`** - Bolt built the `operationId` from the method and the function name. Two routes with one function name, for example `list_items` in two routers, got the same `operationId`. The document was not valid, and client generators merged or rejected the operations. Now the second route gets the suffix `_2`, the third `_3`, and so on. The first route keeps its name.
- **The API key check compares in constant time** - Bolt looked up the API key of a request in a hash set. The comment of the check said that it compared in constant time.
