Now the route decorator raises a `TypeError` that names the handler, the parameter and the type. Each type that converted before still registers. On a `POST`, `PUT` or `PATCH` route, a parameter with no marker of a type that only JSON gives, such as `list[dict]`, `list[list[int]]` or a `TypedDict`, reads the JSON body. Before, it was a query parameter, and a JSON body got 422 "Missing required query parameter". A `dict` parameter of a `GET` route gets the `TypeError` too. `Any` stays a query parameter, as in FastAPI.
- **An empty string for an auth backend option is a configuration error** - `JWTAuthentication` accepted `header=""`, `cookie=""`, `audience=""`, `issuer=""` and `token_type=""`. `APIKeyAuthentication` accepted `header=""`. The backend then rejected each request, or accepted only a token whose claim is an empty string. An unset environment variable, as in `audience=os.environ.get("JWT_AUDIENCE", "")`, gave this result. Now the constructor raises `ImproperlyConfigured`. Use `None` for no value.
- **A router with a parent registers one time** - With `child = Router(prefix="/users", parent=parent)`, `api.include_router(parent)` plus `api.include_router(child)` registered each route of the child two times: at `/v1/users/me` and at `/users/me`. `api.include_router(child)` alone gave `/users/me`, with the middleware of the parent but not its prefix. `parent.include_router(child)` for a router made with `parent=parent` also added the child two times. Now `include_router()` registers a router with a parent one time for each `prefix`, with the prefixes of its parents: `/v1/users/me`. The order of the calls has no effect. A router that you include with two different prefixes still registers two times.
- **`Token` checks its time claims and its extra claims at construction** - `Token(nbf=123)` or an `iat` that is not a datetime was accepted, and `to_dict()` or `encode()` failed later with `AttributeError`. An entry of `extras` with the name of a Token field, such as `exp`, `sub` or `is_staff`, replaced the value of that field in the encoded token. Thus `Token.create(sub, exp=...)` made a token with another expiry than its `exp` field. Now a wrong type raises `TypeError`, and such an extra raises `ValueError`. `Token.create()` sets the fields `jti`, `nbf` and `is_superuser` from keyword arguments of these names.
- **A response status is from 200 to 599** - A handler could send a 1xx status or a status from 600 to 999, for example `JSON(data, status_code=700)`, `HTTPException(status_code=100)` or `@api.get("/x", status_code=150)`. The status went to the client as it was. A 1xx status is interim and is not the status of a response, and no status from 600 has a meaning (RFC 9110 section 15). Now a status outside 200 to 599 from a handler gives a 500, and the Rust log names the bad status. A `status_code=` of a route outside this range raises `ValueError` at the decorator.
- **`JWTAuthViews(prefix="/")` gives the refresh cookie the path `/`** - With `prefix="/"` or `prefix=""`, the routes are at the root, and the refresh cookie had an empty `Path` attribute. A browser then uses the directory of the request URL, which is not a value that Bolt selects. Now the refresh cookie and the cookie that clears it have `Path=/`. Other prefixes keep their path.
- **A response type of `dict`, `list` or `list[Serializer]` refuses a result of another kind** - With `-> list[UserSerializer]`, `return 3` answered 200 with the body `3`, and a `str` or a `dict` result passed too. With `-> dict`, `return "abc"` gave the JSON `"abc"` from a handler with no `await`, and `text/plain` from each other handler. `bytes` gave base64 JSON or `application/octet-stream` in the same way. Now a `list[Serializer]` route answers with a response validation error for a result that is not a list, and `None` is the empty list, as for `list[Struct]`. A `-> dict`, `-> list`, `-> str` or `-> bytes` route answers with a response validation error for a `str` or `bytes` result that its type does not name. A route with `validate_response=False` sends a `str` as `text/plain` and `bytes` as `application/octet-stream` from each kind of handler. A `@paginate` method with `@action` now has the pagination envelope as its response type, as a paginated route has.
- **A page of a `Serializer` type validates its items** - `@paginate` on a handler with the return type `list[Row]`, where `Row` is a Bolt `Serializer`, took the kind of all items from the first item. For a page that starts with a dict, it read each field with `item.get(field)`. Thus a dict with no required field gave `null` with status 200, a dict got `null` where the serializer has a default, and an object after a dict gave a 500 with `AttributeError`. Now each item goes through the serializer as the same route with no `@paginate` sends it: Bolt validates a dict, keeps a serializer instance, and reads each other object with `from_model()`. An item that does not fit gives the same response validation error (status 500). A `values()` QuerySet must now select each required field of the serializer.
- **A nested `Router` inherits `auth`, `guards` and `tags` of its parent router** - A child router took only the middleware of its parent. A route of the child was open when only the parent set `guards`. Now the child takes each value that it does not set from the nearest parent router that sets it. A value of the child router or of the route wins.
- **A dict or a list `detail` of an `HTTPException` is JSON in the body** - `HTTPException(400, detail={"code": "x"})` sent `{"detail": "{'code': 'x'}"}`. The detail went through `str()`, so the client got the text of a Python repr. Now a dict or a list goes to the body as a JSON value: `{"detail": {"code": "x"}}`. `exc.detail` and the `detail` of the error formatter hold the dict or the list. A detail of another type still becomes its text, and an empty detail still gives the phrase of the status.
- **A view decorator on a handler with no `request` parameter stops registration** - `@csrf_exempt` below the route decorator on a handler with no parameter answered 500 for each request. The wrapper of a Django view decorator takes `request` as its first argument, and Bolt calls it with the arguments of the handler. Registration now raises a `TypeError` that says the handler must take `request` as its first parameter.
- **The options of `Body()` have an effect** - `Body()` took `embed`, `alias`, `description` and `example`, and nothing read them. With `Body(embed=True)`, Bolt still read the value from the whole body, so `{"item": {...}}` got 422. Now `embed=True` reads the value from the key of the parameter name in a JSON object, and `alias` sets that key. A default applies to a request with no body and to an object with no such key. The OpenAPI document shows the wrapper object, the `description` and the `example` of the request body. `Form(example=...)` is now in the schema of the form field. Without `embed`, `alias` has no effect, as in FastAPI.
- **The write actions of a ViewSet give the 422 body of a function route** - For one bad body, a function route and a ViewSet gave two different 422 bodies. A function route reports the first error, with the type `missing_field` for a missing field. The generated `create` and `update` actions reported each error, with the type `missing`. `partial_update` reported each bad value. Now the three actions decode the body with the body extractor of the framework. They give the same 422 body as a function route with the same serializer: the first error, with the type `missing_field` for a missing field. `Serializer.model_validate()` and `model_validate_json()` do not change: they still collect each error.
- **An unknown `samesite` value of a cookie raises `ValueError`** - `set_cookie(..., samesite="bogus")` and `Cookie(..., samesite="bogus")` sent `SameSite=Lax`, with no error. Rust read only `strict` and `none`, and each other value became `Lax`. Now the value must be `"lax"`, `"none"` or `"strict"` in any letter case, or a false value to omit the attribute. Another value raises `ValueError('samesite must be "lax", "none", or "strict".')` when the cookie is set, as in Django.
- **A `websocket.close` message takes `None` for the reason, and reserved close codes are refused** - A mounted ASGI app that sent `{"type": "websocket.close", "reason": None}` got a `TypeError`, and the client got close code 1011. The ASGI specification permits `None`. Bolt now sends an empty reason. `close()` also accepted 1004 and 1016 to 2999, which RFC 6455 reserves. `close()`, the server and `WebSocketTestClient` now accept 1000 to 1003, 1007 to 1014 and 3000 to 4999, as the `websockets` library does.
- **A Django middleware sees the scheme of the Bolt request** - Behind a proxy in `BOLT_TRUSTED_PROXIES` that sends `X-Forwarded-Proto: https`, `request.build_absolute_uri()` of the handler gave an `https` URL. But the Django request of a Django middleware had `request.scheme == "http"` and `request.is_secure() == False`, unless `SECURE_PROXY_SSL_HEADER` was also set. Thus `SecurityMiddleware` with `SECURE_SSL_REDIRECT` sent a redirect for a request that came over TLS. Now the Django request reads the scheme of the Bolt request, as a Django request reads it from a WSGI or ASGI server. `SECURE_PROXY_SSL_HEADER` still applies on top, as in Django. `request.META["wsgi.url_scheme"]` holds the scheme.
- **`create_serializer()` gives a ForeignKey that `to_model()` can save, and a `Decimal` for a `DecimalField`** - A `ForeignKey` or `OneToOneField` became an `int` field, the primary key. `to_model()` and `update_instance()` gave that `int` to the relation, so Django raised `ValueError: Cannot assign "1": "BlogPost.author" must be a "Author" instance`. The create and update actions of a `ModelViewSet` call these methods. Now they write a primary key to the column attribute, such as `author_id`. A model instance still goes to the relation. This applies to each Serializer with such a field. A `DecimalField` became a `float` field, which loses digits. Now it is a `Decimal` field. Its JSON output is a string such as `"19.99"`, not a number. The input can be a string or a number.
- **The `status_code` of a ViewSet class applies to its `@action` routes** - A class with `status_code = 202` answered 202 for the standard actions and 200 for an `@action` route. Now an action with no `status_code` uses the value of `@api.viewset(...)`, then the class attribute.
- **`WebSocketTestClient` refuses a send after the close** - A mounted ASGI app that sent `websocket.send` after its own `websocket.close` got no error in the test client. On the server that send raises `ConnectionResetError`. The test client now raises the same error. The WebSocket scope of the test client also has no `auth_context` key now. The server scope has no such key, and nothing read it.
- **A `bool` key of `response_model` stops registration** - `response_model={True: A, 201: A}` registered, because `True` is an `int` in Python. The key counted as the status code 1. Now a `bool` key raises `TypeError` at registration.
- **A subclass of `int`, `float`, `bytes` or `timedelta` encodes as its base type** - msgspec encodes only the exact types, and Bolt had no encoder for a subclass. A response with such a value raised `TypeError: Unsupported type`, and the client got a 500. Now the encoder gives msgspec a value of the exact base type, so the JSON is the same as for the base type.
- **A `bytes` field of a parameter model gets the UTF-8 bytes of its text value** - A `bytes` field of a `Query()`, `Form()`, `Header()` or `Cookie()` model read its text as base64, and a plain `bytes` parameter did not. Text that was not base64 gave a 422. The `bytes` item of a `tuple[bytes, int]` parameter also read base64. Each now gets the UTF-8 bytes of the text, as a plain `bytes` parameter and as FastAPI do. A `bytes` field of a JSON body continues to read base64.
- **`field(alias=...)` of a Serializer is the name of the field in JSON** - The docstring said that the alias is the name in JSON input and output. But the input accepted only the field name, and the output had the alias only with `dump(by_alias=True)`. With `user_name: str = field(alias="userName")`, a body `{"userName": "a"}` got 422. Now the alias is the key in a request body, in `model_validate()`, in `dump()` and in a response, as with `Field(alias=...)` in pydantic and FastAPI. The input does not accept the field name any more. The alias wins over the `rename` option of the class. `by_alias` of `dump()` has no effect, because a field has one name in JSON.
- **A `response_model` dict with no 2xx code keeps the default status 200** - With `response_model={404: Err, ...: str}`, a bare `return "hello"` answered 404. The default status of the route was the lowest code of the dict when the dict had no 2xx code. Now such a dict does not change the default status. A bare return answers 200, or the `status_code` of the decorator. A dict with a 2xx code still makes its lowest 2xx code the default, as the docs say.
- **`process_exception` of a Django middleware runs** - Bolt did not call `process_exception` when a handler raised an exception. `DjangoMiddleware`, `DjangoMiddlewareStack` and `django_middleware=[...]` now call the hook of each middleware, the last middleware first, as Django does. The first response that a hook returns answers the request. If no hook returns a response, the Bolt error handlers answer as before. An `HTTPException` and a validation error do not go to the hook.
- **A route with the response type `dict` can return a response object** - A handler with `-> dict` that returned `JSON(..., status_code=201)`, `Redirect(...)` or another response object answered 500. The same was true for a `list[...]` or `dict[...]` type with `validate_response=False`. Now the response object goes out, as for a route with no response type.
- **`create_jwt_for_user()` refuses an extra claim that replaces a standard claim** - `extra_claims` was merged last, so `extra_claims={"is_superuser": True}` or `{"sub": "1"}` replaced the claim of the user. Now `sub`, `exp`, `iat`, `is_staff` or `is_superuser` in `extra_claims` raises `ValueError`. Other claims, such as `jti` and `permissions`, work as before. **Behavior change:** code that set one of these five claims through `extra_claims` now gets the error.
- **`subset()` keeps the model validators that it can run** - `Serializer.subset()` copied the field validators of its fields, but no `@model_validator`. A body that the parent rejects, such as `low` above `high`, passed for the subset with status 200. Now the subset gets each model validator of the parent that names only fields of the subset. A model validator that names a field outside the subset stays out, because the subset does not have that attribute.
- **A wrong method on the other slash form of a route path answers 405** - With only `GET /x`, `GET /x/` redirected to `/x`, but `POST /x/` answered 404, because the redirect check is for each method. Now a path whose other slash form has routes answers 405, and `Allow` lists the methods of the route path. An `OPTIONS` request to that path gets the automatic 204 with the same `Allow`. A method of the route still gets the 308 redirect.
- **`BoltAPI(compression=...)` refuses a value of a wrong type** - `compression=True`, a dict or a string was stored as it was. The server then stopped at startup with `AttributeError: ... has no attribute to_rust_config`. Now `BoltAPI()` raises `TypeError` for a value that is not a `CompressionConfig`, `None` or `False`.
- **`problem_details` sends a string as `"detail"`** - A `dict` or a `list` detail of an `HTTPException` was the `"detail"` member of the Problem Details body. RFC 9457 specifies a string there. Now the keys of a `dict` detail are extension members, and a `list` detail is the `"extra"` member, by the rules of `error.extra`. `"detail"` is then the title of the status. **Behavior change:** a client of an API with `problem_details` that read a `dict` from `"detail"` now reads the extension members.
- **`DjangoMiddlewareStack` takes the dotted path of a middleware class** - `DjangoMiddlewareStack(["django.contrib.sessions.middleware.SessionMiddleware"])` raised no error, but each request answered 500 with `'str' object is not callable`. `DjangoMiddleware` already imports a dotted path. Now the stack imports it too.
- **`process_response` of a Django middleware gets the error response of a handler** - When a handler raised an exception, Bolt made the error response after the Django middleware chain. Thus `SessionMiddleware` did not save the session, and a middleware that adds a header did not add it. Bolt now makes the error response inside the chain, as Django does, after the `process_exception` hooks. `get_response` returns the error response and does not raise. In a list of `DjangoMiddleware` wrappers, `process_exception` of each wrapper now runs before the error response, and the response goes through `process_response` of each wrapper.
- **`Config.read_only` with a plain default is the same as `field(read_only=True, default=...)`** - With `forbid_unknown_fields=True`, a `Config.read_only` field such as `id: int = 0` accepted an input value equal to its default, and rejected each other value. The serializer now gives the field the marker default of `field()` at class creation. Thus each input value is an unknown field, as with `field(read_only=True, default=0)`.
- **`Config.read_only` for a field of a base class refuses each input value** - With `forbid_unknown_fields=True`, a `Config.read_only` field of a base class accepted an input value equal to its default. A field with a `msgspec.field()` default did the same. Now each input value for such a field is an error. If the serializer cannot get the type of a base class field at class creation, class creation raises `ImproperlyConfigured`. This occurs when the annotation has a name that is not defined yet, or a type parameter with no type argument. It also occurs when the field is positional in one class and keyword-only in the other. In these cases, declare the field in the subclass with `field(read_only=True, default=...)`. (#870)
- **A `timedelta` parameter converts its value** - A path, query, header, cookie or form parameter of the type `datetime.timedelta` gave the handler the text of the request, such as `"PT5S"`. A `list[timedelta]` parameter converted its items. The parameter now gives a `timedelta`, and text that is not a duration answers 422.
- **The test clients remove the spaces around a request header value** - An HTTP server removes the spaces and tabs around a header value. `TestClient`, `AsyncTestClient` and `WebSocketTestClient` sent the value as the test gave it. Thus `X-API-Key: " key1"` got a 401 in a test and a 200 from `runbolt`, and an API key that starts with a space worked only in a test. Now the test clients remove these characters, as the server does.
- **A `-> dict` or `-> list` route refuses each result of another kind** - A `-> dict` route sent a list or a number as JSON, and a `-> list` route sent a dict, with no error. A `-> dict` route now takes a `dict`. A `-> list` route takes a `list`, a `tuple` or a QuerySet. Each other result is a response validation error. A tuple of a `-> list` route was an error and is now a JSON array. A route with `validate_response=False` does no such check.
- **The OpenAPI document shows a `bytes` parameter as plain text** - A `bytes` path, query, header, cookie or form parameter, and a `bytes` field of a `Query()`, `Form()`, `Header()` or `Cookie()` model, showed `contentEncoding: base64`. Bolt gives such a value as the UTF-8 bytes of its text, so the schema is now `{"type": "string"}`. A `bytes` field of a JSON body keeps `contentEncoding: base64`, and a `bytes` file part keeps `format: binary`.
- **The access log has a line for a request that Bolt rejects before the handler** - The `django.server` access log of `runbolt` had no line for a response that Rust gives before the handler runs. Thus a 401 or a 403 from authentication or a guard, a 429 from a rate limit, and a 422 from path or query validation were not in the log. Now each response of a matched route writes its line, at `WARNING` for a 4xx. The 308 redirect to the other slash form of a route path and the automatic `OPTIONS` answer also write a line, at `INFO`. With an `error_formatter`, the line has the status of the formatted response. A request with no route, a mount and a native route write no line, as before.
- **A subclass of `bytearray` in a response encodes as a `bytearray`** - A response with a `bytearray` value is base64 text in JSON. A value of a subclass of `bytearray` gave a 500 with `TypeError: Unsupported type`. Now it encodes as base64 text too.
- **`WebSocketException` refuses a code or a reason that makes an invalid close frame** - A handler could raise `WebSocketException` or `WebSocketClose` with a reserved code such as 1005, or with a reason of more than 123 bytes of UTF-8. Bolt then sent that close frame. The exception now does the check of `WebSocket.close()` and raises `ValueError` when the handler makes it. The server also refuses such a code or reason on a raised exception and closes with 1011.
- **`sample_rate` and `min_duration_ms` of `LoggingConfig` have an effect** - The docs give the two options to make the access log smaller in production. Only `LoggingMiddleware.log_response()` read them, and nothing calls it, so the access log of `runbolt` wrote each response. The settings `DJANGO_BOLT_LOG_SAMPLE` and `DJANGO_BOLT_LOG_SLOW_MS` had no effect for the same reason. Now the Rust access log applies them to the 1xx to 3xx responses of each route of the API: `BoltAPI(logging_config=LoggingConfig(sample_rate=0.05, min_duration_ms=250))`. An API with no `logging_config` takes the two settings. A 4xx or a 5xx response always writes its line. Bolt reads the values once, at route registration, and Rust applies them with no Python call. A value out of range stops startup: `ValueError` for a `LoggingConfig`, `ImproperlyConfigured` for a setting. Before, a wrong setting value was ignored. **Behavior change:** the default config had `min_duration_ms=250` when `DEBUG = False`, with no effect. Bolt removes this default, so a project with neither option keeps each line. Bolt no longer reads `DJANGO_BOLT_LOG_LEVEL`. It had no effect and no documentation. Set the level of the `django.server` logger in `LOGGING`.
- **The admin mount serves an admin site in `i18n_patterns()`** - With `i18n_patterns(path("admin/", admin.site.urls))`, `runbolt` mounted the admin only at the prefix of the default language. Thus `/en/admin/` worked, and `/fr/admin/` and `/admin/` answered 404. Now `runbolt` mounts the prefix of each language of `settings.LANGUAGES`. It also mounts the path with no language, so Django's `LocaleMiddleware` redirects `/admin/` to the prefix of a language. With `prefix_default_language=False`, the default language has no prefix. An admin site behind a path parameter, such as `path("<tenant>/admin/", admin.site.urls)`, cannot be a mount. `runbolt` mounted `/admin` with no message, and each admin path answered 404. Now `runbolt` prints a warning that names `api.mount_django("/", clear_root_path=True)` as the way to serve it.
- **A `(status, response object)` tuple sends the response object with the status of the tuple** - On a route with a `response_model` dict, `return 404, JSON({"detail": "no"})` gave a 500 with `TypeError: Handler returned unsupported type 'JSON'`. Now Bolt sends the response object with the status of the tuple. The body, the headers and the cookies come from the response object. The response object of the handler keeps its own status.
- **`settings.BOLT_COMPRESSION` refuses a value of a wrong type** - `BOLT_COMPRESSION = True` or a dict stopped `runbolt` and `TestClient` with `AttributeError: 'bool' object has no attribute 'to_rust_config'`. The error did not name the setting. Now such a value raises `ImproperlyConfigured`, and the message names `settings.BOLT_COMPRESSION` and the type. The setting and the `compression` argument of `BoltAPI` use one check.
- **A `response_model` key that is not an integer or `...` stops registration** - A key such as `"404"`, `404.0` or `None` matched no status. Thus the schema of that key had no effect on the response and was not in the OpenAPI document, and nothing reported it. Now such a key raises `TypeError` at the decorator, as a `bool` key does.
- **`skip_paths` and `skip_status_codes` of `LoggingConfig` have an effect** - The docs give the two options to remove lines from the log. Only `LoggingMiddleware.log_request()` and `log_response()` read them, and nothing calls these, so the access log of `runbolt` wrote each response. Now the Rust access log applies them to each route of the API: `BoltAPI(logging_config=LoggingConfig(skip_paths={"/health"}, skip_status_codes={204}))`. A request with a path in `skip_paths` writes no line. The match is exact, on the complete request path. A response with a status in `skip_status_codes` writes no line. The two options apply to each status class. Bolt reads the values once, at route registration, and Rust applies them with no Python call. A value of a wrong type stops startup with `ValueError`. **Behavior change:** the default `skip_paths` is `/health`, `/ready` and `/metrics`, as the docs say. Thus these paths write no access-log line now. Use `LoggingConfig(skip_paths=set())` to log them. The docs now say that the field options, the obfuscation options and `log_request_body` do not change the access-log line.
- **A `Header()` or `Cookie()` model is one parameter for each field in the OpenAPI document** - A header or cookie model was one parameter with the name of the handler argument and an object schema. No client sends such a header. A `Query()` model was already one parameter for each field. Now each field of a header or cookie model is its own parameter, with `required`, the schema and the default of the field. The name is the name that Bolt reads: a header field is in lower case with `-` for each `_`, for example `x-request-id`.
- **A `deque`, a generator, a `Fraction` or a compiled pattern in a response is JSON** - `return {"recent": collections.deque([1, 2])}` gave a 500 with `TypeError: Unsupported type`. A subclass of `deque` did the same. Now a `deque` and a generator are a JSON array, and their items go through the encoder again. A `fractions.Fraction` is text, such as `"1/3"`. A compiled `re.Pattern` is its pattern text. pydantic gives the same JSON for each of them.
- **The access log writes one 429 line for each second** - After the access log got a line for each response of a route, a flood of rejected requests wrote one `WARNING` line for each 429. A client could thus fill the log. The line of the rate limiter on stderr already has a limit. Now each worker thread writes the access-log line of the first 429 response of each second. The next 429 line of that worker ends with `| suppressed: N`, the number of 429 responses that wrote no line. The limit applies to each 429 response of a route. Other status codes are not limited.
- **The admin in `i18n_patterns()` uses the default language that Django serves** - With `LANGUAGE_CODE = "en-us"`, the default of Django, and only `"en"` in `LANGUAGES`, Django serves the default language at `/en/admin/`. Bolt took the prefix from `LANGUAGE_CODE`. Thus the `runbolt` banner showed `/en-us/admin/`, which Django answers with 404, and Bolt made a mount at that path. Now Bolt reads the variant of `LANGUAGE_CODE` that `LANGUAGES` has, as `LocaleMiddleware` does. The banner shows `/en/admin/`, and `/en-us/admin` is not a mount. With `prefix_default_language=False`, the default language keeps the path with no prefix.
- **A path, header or cookie list refuses a default of two or more items** - `tags: Annotated[list[str], Header()] = ["a", "b"]` had `maxItems: 1` together with `default: ["a", "b"]` in the OpenAPI document. Thus the default was not a valid value of its own schema. `maxItems: 1` is true: Bolt keeps the last line of a repeated header, and it does not split a value at a comma. A cookie name and a path segment also give one value. Now such a default raises `TypeError` when the route registers. The error names the parameter and the default. The same rule applies to `Path()`, `Cookie()` and to a sequence field of a `Header()` or `Cookie()` model. For a model field, the check uses the default that the OpenAPI document shows. A `Query()` list keeps each default, because a query key can repeat. (#871)
- **A list parameter with an empty default has a valid OpenAPI schema** - `tags: Annotated[list[str], Header()] = []` had `minItems: 1` together with `default: []` in the document. The same was true for `Cookie()`. Thus the default was not a valid value of its own schema, and an OpenAPI validator refused the document. Now a header or cookie list with an empty default has no `minItems`. An empty `tuple`, `set` or `frozenset` default also has no `minItems`. `maxItems: 1` stays, because a header or a cookie gives one value.
- **A constrained sequence field of a header or cookie model takes the value** - A `Header()` or `Cookie()` model field `tags: Annotated[list[str], msgspec.Meta(max_length=1)]` gave a 422 with "Expected `array`, got `str`" for a valid request. Now the field gets a list of one item, as a field with no constraint does.
- **A `response_model` key outside 200 to 599 stops registration** - `response_model={200: Item, 700: Err}` and `{99: Err, 200: Item}` registered. A response status is from 200 to 599, so no response could use such a key. The key was in the OpenAPI document as a status that the route cannot send. Now an integer key outside 200 to 599 raises `ValueError` at the decorator.
- **The access log has a line for a 405** - A request with a method that a route path does not have answers 405. This answer wrote no line in the `django.server` access log. Now it writes a line at `WARNING`, as each other 4xx of a route does.
- **`/auth/logout` of `JWTAuthViews` keeps the session revoked when a refresh runs at the same moment** - The family entry lived only as long as the old refresh token, so a token that a concurrent refresh gave worked again after the entry expired. The entry now lives for the full lifetime of a new refresh token.
- **`rotate_refresh_token` keeps a used refresh token blocked until `exp + leeway`** - The store got the `exp` of the token, so a store that keeps an entry until that `exp` let a used refresh token work a second time in the leeway window of `JWTAuthentication`. The consumed entry and the family entry now get `exp + leeway`.
- **`runbolt` stops with exit status 1 when the project has no `BoltAPI`** - It printed a warning and stopped with exit status 0, and with more than one process it started workers that stopped at once. It now stops with a `CommandError` before it starts a worker.
- **A worker thread gives its pooled connection back after a sync request** - A sync handler that ran on the worker thread and queried a pooled database through a helper left the connection on that thread. An idle worker thread then held one pool slot, and calls on other threads failed with a pool timeout. The worker thread now gives the connection back when the request ends.
- **`JWTAuthentication.get_user` recovers from a dead database connection and runs on the lane of the request** - The async `get_user` ran its query on the default executor of the event loop. After the database closed the connection of that thread, each load on it failed until the process restarted, and on a route with Django middleware the query did not see the tenant schema of the request. The query now runs through `run_in_orm_executor`, on the lane of the request or on the ORM pool.
- **`runbolt --respawn-failed-workers` stops with exit status 1 when a worker cannot start** - A worker that failed before it listened on the port (port in use, import error) was replaced each second, without end. Now `runbolt` stops all workers and reports the failure. A worker that fails after it started to serve is replaced as before.
- **An optional field of a parameter model has the schema of an optional parameter** - `opt: str | None = None` in a `Form()` model gave `{"type": "string", "default": null}` in the OpenAPI document. That default is not valid for the type, and a client generator can refuse it. The same field in a `Query()` model gave `anyOf` with a null arm, but a query value cannot be null. Now a `T | None` field of a `Query()`, `Header()`, `Cookie()` or `Form()` model gives the schema of `T` with no `None` default, as a parameter with no model does. `required: false` says that the field is optional. A default that is not `None` stays. A union of two types or more keeps each arm.
- **A `-> None` route validates its result** - With `-> None`, a handler that returned a `dict`, a `list`, a Struct or a number got status 200 with that value as the body. Now a result that is not `None` is a response validation error, as a wrong result is for each other response type. A `None` result is still `null`, or an empty body with status 204. A response object, such as `JSON(...)`, `Response(...)` or a redirect, has its own body and is not validated on a `-> None` route. A route with `validate_response=False` does not change.
- **The admin mount serves an admin site behind a path parameter** - With `path("<tenant>/admin/", site.urls)`, `runbolt` mounted `/admin`, and each admin path answered 404. Now `runbolt` mounts Django at the root path for this URL pattern, as it does for an admin site at the root path. Django gets each request that no Bolt route matches, so `/acme/admin/` reaches the admin. `detect_admin_url_prefix()` returns `""` for this URL pattern. Before, it returned `"admin"`. A Django mount at `/` of your own stays, and `runbolt` adds no second mount. A mount at `/` and a Bolt route at `/` can now exist together. Before, `runbolt` and `TestClient` raised `ValueError` for them. A request with a method that a route path does not have now gets 405 from Bolt, as the docs say. Before, a mount that matched the path got it. (#872)
- **A response does not change the `headers=` dict of the caller** - `Response`, `JSON`, `PlainText`, `HTML`, `Redirect`, `File` and `FileResponse` kept the `headers=` dict by reference. A write to `response.headers` changed the dict of the caller. A middleware that set a header on a response with a module-level dict put the header into each later response that used the dict. Now each response copies the dict in its constructor, as `StreamingResponse` did. (#867)

### Changed

- **A WebSocket upgrade with a value that fails the check of its parameter gets 400** - Rust refused a value that did not convert with 400. A value that a Python check refused gave 500, for example an item of a `list[int]`, a `Query(ge=1)` limit or a field of a parameter model. Now each one gets 400 before the handler runs, with a detail such as ``Query parameter 'ids': Invalid integer 'abc': ... - at `$[1]` ``. `WebSocketTestClient` raises `ValueError` with this detail, as for a value that does not convert. A `RequestValidationError` that the handler raises before `accept()` also gets 400, also when an error item has no `"msg"`. Before, the item `5.0` of a `list[int]` was accepted with 101. Now it gets 400, as a single `int` does.
- **BREAKING: An aware `datetime` argument keeps the UTC offset of its text** - Bolt changed `2024-01-15T10:30:00+05:30` to `2024-01-15T05:00:00+00:00`. The instant was correct, but `.hour` and `.tzinfo` were not those of the text. Now the handler gets `10:30:00+05:30`, as `datetime.fromisoformat()` gives. A text with `Z`, `+00:00` or `-00:00` gives `datetime.timezone.utc`. A text with no offset still gives a naive `datetime`. This applies to path, query, header, cookie and form values, to the fields of a parameter model, and to WebSocket path and query values. (audit finding F1)
- **BREAKING: A `date`, `time` or `datetime` argument refuses text that is not ISO 8601** - chrono read fields of any width and skipped whitespace before a number. Thus `24-01-15` was the year 24. Now Rust reads the ISO 8601 forms with fixed field widths, and these texts get a 422 (audit finding F2):
  - A field of another width, such as `24-01-15`, `2024-1-5`, `1:2:3` or `2024-01-15T10:30:0`.
  - A sign before the year, as in `+2024-01-15`.
  - Whitespace before a field, as in `\t10:30:00` or `2024-01-15T 10:30:00`, and two characters or none between the date and the time, as in `2024-01-15  10:30:00` or `2024-01-1510:30:00`.
  - A lower-case `z` for UTC in a `datetime`, as in `2024-01-15T10:30:00z`.
  Python `fromisoformat()` refuses each of these texts. Rust still parses each value before the GIL, with no Python call.
- **An `int`, `date`, `time` or `datetime` argument accepts the other forms that Python reads** - Bolt now reads the text as `int()` and `fromisoformat()` read it on Python 3.12, 3.13 and 3.14. These texts now convert:
  - An `int` beyond the 64-bit range gives a Python `int` (audit finding F3). It can have as many digits as `sys.get_int_max_str_digits()` allows, at most 4300. With the limit 0 (no limit in Python), Bolt uses 4300. Bolt reads the limit when the server starts. A longer text gets a 422, also when it starts with zeros. A process has one limit, as Python has one for each interpreter, so the last server or test client that starts sets it.
  - A `datetime` with no seconds, as in `2024-01-15T10:30`, which an HTML `<input type="datetime-local">` sends. Also an hour with no minutes, an offset with no colon (`+0530`) or with hours only (`+05`), a `t` separator, the basic format (`20240115T103000`), week dates (`2024-W03-1T10:30`) and a comma before the fraction (audit finding F4).
  - A `date` in the basic format (`20240115`) or as a week date (`2024-W03-1`).
  - A `time` with an offset, such as `10:30:00+05:00` or `10:30:00Z`, gives an aware `time` (audit finding F5). A `time` can also have hours only (`10`), the basic format (`103000`) or a leading `T`.
  Bolt refuses some texts that Python reads, as before: `_`, whitespace and digits that are not ASCII in a number, a separator other than `T`, `t` or whitespace between the date and the time, `24:00`, a fraction of an hour or a minute, seconds in an offset (`+05:30:15`, also `+05:30:00`), and text between the time and the offset. RFC 3339 has no seconds in an offset, and the JSON encoder writes an offset in whole minutes. The 422 detail now names the expected format, for example `Invalid integer 'abc': expected a whole number such as 42 or -7` and `Invalid time '1:2:3': expected ISO 8601 format (e.g., 10:30 or 10:30:00+05:30)`. A `float` reads the same text as before. See [Requests](docs/src/topics/requests.md#send-typed-values-as-text).
- **BREAKING: A `list[int]`, `list[float]`, `list[date]`, `list[time]` or `list[datetime]` argument reads each item as a single argument does** - msgspec read each item, so a single argument and a list of one type accepted different text. Now the Rust parser of a single value reads each item. msgspec checks the `msgspec.Meta` constraints. It checks the length of a bounded sequence first, so Rust reads no item of a sequence that is too long. This applies to a `list`, `set`, `frozenset`, `tuple[T]` and `tuple[T, ...]` of one of these types, in a parameter and in a field of a parameter model. Other containers do not change: msgspec still reads the items of `tuple[int, int]` or `list[int | None]`, so `5.0` converts and `+5` gets a 422. These items now get a 422, as `int()` and `fromisoformat()` refuse them: `5.0` and `1e3` for an `int`, a Unix timestamp such as `1700000000` for a `datetime`, and a lower-case `z`. A NUL character, as in `2024-01-15T10:30:00\0`, also gets a 422, although Python reads the text before the NUL. A fraction of more than six digits keeps its first six digits; msgspec rounded it. `20240115` for a `datetime` is a date; msgspec read it as a timestamp. The other forms above now convert, and so do `+5`, `007`, `.5`, `5.` and `1e999`. The error of a bad item names the format, for example ``Invalid integer 'x': expected a whole number such as 42 or -7 - at `$[1]` ``. When an item does not convert and an earlier item breaks a constraint, the 422 now names the item that does not convert. For example, `ids=11&ids=x` with `list[Annotated[int, Meta(le=10)]]` gave ``Expected `int` <= 10 - at `$[0]` ``, and now gives ``Invalid integer 'x': ... - at `$[1]` ``.
- **BREAKING: a route that can never get a request stops the startup, and the error names the routes** - A conflict gave only the path of the earlier route in the syntax of the router, for example `Insertion failed due to conflict with previously registered route: /files/{*file_path}`. Now the message names both routes with the method and the path as you wrote them, and gives the fix: `GET /files/{file_id} conflicts with GET /files/{file_path:path}. Keep one of the two routes, or add a fixed segment before the parameter in one path.` A dynamic route at the method and path of another route gives `GET /items/{id} is already registered`, as a static route does. A path with a `{name:path}` parameter before the end, two parameters in one segment, or empty braces gives an error with the method and the path in front. WebSocket routes give the same messages, with the method `WEBSOCKET`. Two WebSocket routes at one path, also `/live` and `/live/`, now stop the startup. Before, the second handler replaced the first with no sign. A path with a `{` and no `}`, such as `/files/{id`, now stops the startup. Before, it registered, and Bolt did not read the parameter from the path. A handler with an `id` argument got 422, unless the query had an `id`. `runbolt` now stops with `CommandError` for an error of a WebSocket route, as for an HTTP route. Thus an app with one of these WebSocket routes or paths does not start. See [Routes that stop the startup](docs/src/topics/routing.md#routes-that-stop-the-startup). (audit finding F6)
- **BREAKING: `runbolt` refuses some `FORCE_SCRIPT_NAME` values that Django serves** - Bolt puts the script name in paths and redirects as it is. Thus `runbolt` and the test clients now raise `ImproperlyConfigured` at startup for a script name with a character that Django percent-encodes in a URL. These characters are a space, each character that is not ASCII, and `%`, `;`, `=`, `?`, `#`, `[`, `]`, `|`, `\`, `"`, `<`, `>`, `^`, `` ` ``, `{` and `}`. The characters that stay valid are ASCII letters, digits and `/:@&+$,-_.!~*'()`. Before, `runbolt` started with such a value. They also refuse a `STATIC_URL` or `MEDIA_URL` that you set to the script name, such as `"/myapp/"`, when Bolt serves those files. The proxy removes the script name, so the files would be at `/` and hide each route. Django puts the script name in front of a relative value, and Bolt does not refuse it. With the default `MEDIA_URL` `""`, Bolt serves no media, as without the setting. A test client with a `static_files_config` does not check `STATIC_URL`, because that config replaces it. To migrate, use a script name with only the valid characters, and give the files a path of their own, such as `"static/"`. (#552)
- **BREAKING: with `FORCE_SCRIPT_NAME`, Bolt serves static and media files without the script name** - With `FORCE_SCRIPT_NAME = "/myapp"` and `STATIC_URL = "static/"`, `runbolt` served the files at `/myapp/static/`. A proxy that removes `/myapp` sends `/static/site.css`, and that gave 404. Now Bolt removes the script name from the front of `STATIC_URL` and `MEDIA_URL`, as WhiteNoise does. It serves `/static/site.css`, and `/myapp/static/site.css` gives 404. `{% static %}` still gives `/myapp/static/site.css`. To migrate, let the proxy remove the sub-path from static and media requests too, as from the other requests. (#552)
- **BREAKING: JWT: a token issued in the future fails** - A token whose `iat` is later than now plus the `leeway` of the backend now gets 401, as in PyJWT 2.10 and later. Before, Bolt did not check `iat`. The `leeway` (default 60 seconds) applies to `exp`, `nbf` and `iat`. Thus an issuer whose clock is more than `leeway` seconds fast gets 401. (audit finding J5)
- **BREAKING: JWT: a token expires at the start of the second of its `exp`, plus the `leeway`** - Bolt compared `exp` with the current time in whole seconds. Thus a token passed until the end of that second plus the `leeway`, up to one second longer than PyJWT allows. Now Bolt compares with the current time and its fraction, as PyJWT does. The token fails at `trunc(exp) + leeway`, 60 seconds after the start of the second of `exp` by default. A token now fails up to one second earlier than before. (audit finding J1)
- **JWT: `exp`, `nbf` and `iat` can have a fraction** - RFC 7519 defines a NumericDate as a JSON number, also with a fraction. Bolt refused such a token, and PyJWT accepts it. Now Bolt accepts it too. For the expiry check, Bolt removes the fraction of `exp`, as PyJWT does. It compares `nbf` and `iat` with no rounding. A whole number, also as `N.0` or `Ne0`, is an integer: `auth_claims` gives it as an `int`, and `oat` and `ver` accept it. `auth_claims` gives a time with a fraction as a `float`. Bolt reads a decimal as the nearest float, as Python does. Thus the expiry check reads `exp` = `1795714848.99999985` as `1795714848`, not the next second. `auth_claims` gives the float `1795714848.9999998`. (audit finding J1)
- **BREAKING: JWT: a time claim that a Python `datetime` cannot hold fails the token** - An `exp`, `nbf` or `iat` before the year 1 or after the year 9999 (UTC) now gets 401. Before, Bolt accepted such an integer `exp`, for example `2**62`. Then the refresh and logout routes with `DjangoORMRevocation` gave a 500. PyJWT accepts such a time. An issuer that writes a huge `exp` for "no expiry" must write a time before the year 10000. (audit finding J1)
- **JWT: a claim of another JSON type is malformed and does not fail the token** - Some claims that Bolt types made the token fail with 401 when they had another JSON type. Examples are `"iss": 123` with no `issuer`, `"aud": 123` with no `audience`, `"is_staff": 1` and a string `permissions`. PyJWT accepts each. A private claim of any JSON type passed before too. Now Bolt accepts the token, and a typed claim of another JSON type, also `null`, is malformed. Each reader of a malformed claim fails. Each `Requires` guard on the claim gives its 403, in each form, also `none_of`. For example, `Requires("permissions", none_of=["banned"])` gives 403 for `"permissions": ["banned", 1]` and for `"permissions": "banned"`, as for `["banned"]`. The claim grants nothing: `is_staff` and `is_superuser` are true only for JSON `true`, and `permissions` count only as an array of strings. `auth_claims` gives the claim as `None`. `rotate_refresh_token()` and the refresh route of `JWTAuthViews` refuse a malformed `fam`, `ver`, `oat` or `amr`. The logout routes refuse a malformed `fam` or `ver`. `rotate_refresh_token()` also refuses a `fam` that is not a string with `rotate=False`, and an `amr` that is not a list of strings. With an `issuer` or an `audience`, an `iss` or `aud` of another type still fails the token. See [Give each claim its JSON type](docs/src/topics/authentication.md#give-each-claim-its-json-type). (audit finding J2)
- **BREAKING: JWT: `null` in a typed claim is malformed, not missing** - Before, `null` in `permissions`, `is_staff`, `is_superuser`, `iss`, `aud`, `amr`, `fam`, `oat` or `ver` counted as a missing claim. A `none_of` guard passed it, and a refresh token with such a claim rotated. Now `null` is malformed, as each other value of the wrong JSON type. A `Requires` guard on the claim gives 403, also `none_of`. `rotate_refresh_token()` and the refresh route refuse a `null` `amr`, `fam`, `oat` or `ver`. An issuer that writes `null` for "no value" must leave the claim out. (audit findings J2 and J4)
- **bolt-mcp: the claims from a `token_verifier` follow the same rules** - The bridge read the claims of a verified token with the claim types of Bolt, and a claim of another type gave a 500. This included an `exp` or `iat` with a fraction. Now the bridge accepts a time claim with a fraction. A time claim before the year 1 or after the year 9999 is malformed. A claim of another type is malformed, as on a route: `is_staff` and `is_superuser` grant only for `true`, `permissions` only as a list of strings, and a tool guard on the claim refuses the call. A `sub` of another JSON type now gets 401. A `null` `sub` stays a missing `sub`: `auth_claims` has no `sub`, and a tool guard on `sub` reads it as missing. (audit finding J2)
- **Auth: one or more spaces can follow the scheme** - RFC 9110 allows one or more spaces after an auth scheme. Bolt removed only one, so `Bearer  <token>` with two spaces failed. Now Bolt removes all spaces after `Bearer` or `ApiKey`, for a JWT, an API key and the bearer token of an MCP mount. A tab is not a space, and the token still fails. (audit finding J3)
- **BREAKING: `APIKeyAuthentication` refuses a key that starts with a space** - Bolt now removes all spaces after a scheme, and a header value cannot start with a space. Thus no request can send such a key. Before, `Bearer  <key>` with two spaces sent it. Now such a key raises `ImproperlyConfigured` at startup. The message gives the position of the key in `api_keys`, not the key. (audit finding J3)
- **JWT: Bolt refuses more malformed tokens** - A claim name that occurs two times now fails the token. Before, Bolt kept the last value of a private claim. A duplicate `alg`, `kid`, `crit` or `b64` header parameter also fails. A header that is a JSON array, not an object, now fails. A byte that is not UTF-8 now fails the token, also in a header parameter that Bolt does not read. A `typ`, `token_type` or `token_use` claim that is not a string now fails the token. Before, Bolt ignored such a `token_type` or `token_use`.
- **A URL-encoded form body that is not UTF-8 decodes as Latin-1, as in Django** - Django `request.POST` decodes such a body as Latin-1, as a whole. Bolt gave U+FFFD for each bad byte sequence. Thus `name=caf` and the byte `0xE9` gave `caf` and U+FFFD, and now give `café`. A UTF-8 field of such a body changes too: `é` sent as UTF-8 now gives `Ã©`, as in Django. A key changes in the same way. A UTF-8 body does not change, and an escape such as `%E9` still gives U+FFFD. A `bytes` form argument gets the UTF-8 bytes of the new text. A multipart text field already decoded as in Django, and a file keeps the bytes that the client sent. See [Read form data](docs/src/topics/requests.md#read-form-data). (audit finding F7)
- **BREAKING: `include("django_bolt.urls")` under a prefix moves the routes under the prefix** - With `path("api/", include("django_bolt.urls"))`, `reverse()` gave `/api/articles/42`, but Bolt served `/articles/42`. Now `runbolt` and `TestClient` serve `/api/articles/42`, and `/articles/42` gives 404. The prefix rules of `api.urls` apply, but Bolt ignores the keyword arguments of the include. To keep the old paths, include `django_bolt.urls` with `path("")`. (#552)
- **BREAKING: `runbolt` refuses `django_bolt.urls` under a prefix that it cannot serve** - Examples are `re_path()`, `i18n_patterns()`, a converter other than `str` and a prefix with no trailing slash. Bolt cannot serve such a prefix at static paths. Before, Bolt served the routes at their own paths, and `reverse()` gave other paths. Now `runbolt` and the test clients raise `ImproperlyConfigured`, and the message names the URLconf entry. Django alone does not change: `manage.py check`, `migrate`, `reverse()` and the Django views work, and `reverse()` gives the same paths as before. `re_path(r"^", ...)` at the root still works in `runbolt`. To migrate, use `path()` with a static prefix that ends with a slash, outside `i18n_patterns()`. (#552)
- **`django_bolt.urls.urlpatterns` is a list with one include** - It had one pattern for each named route. Django now reads these patterns after it imports the whole URLconf, so autodiscovery runs at the first `reverse()`, not at the import. A URLconf can still copy the list with `[*urlpatterns]` or join it with `+`. When `ROOT_URLCONF` changes, for example with `override_settings`, the patterns follow the new URLconf. (#552)
- **`TestClient(api)` imports `ROOT_URLCONF`** - It finds the `api.urls` mounts of the API there. A URLconf that does not import now makes `TestClient`, `AsyncTestClient` and `WebSocketTestClient` raise, also for an API that the URLconf does not mount. Such a URLconf already makes the system checks of `runbolt` fail. (#552)
- **A missing field in a nested struct has the type `missing_field` and its own `loc`** - A 422 error for a missing field in a nested struct had the type `validation_error`. Its `loc` ended at the parent, for example `["body", "address"]`. Only the `msg` named the field. A missing field at the top level already had `missing_field` and its name in `loc`. Now a nested missing field gives `missing_field` at any depth, as pydantic does. Its `loc` ends with the field name, for example `["body", "address", "zip"]`. The `msg` names the field and has no path. An array index in `loc` stays a string, for example `["body", "items", "0", "zip"]`. `Serializer.model_validate` gives the same `loc` for a nested field. Its type there stays `missing`.
- **A parameter with an unknown source stops the route registration** - A `Param` marker with a source that Bolt does not know, such as `Param(source="cookies")`, registered with no error. Each request then gave a 422, or used the default with no error. Now the registration raises `TypeError` and names the parameter and the source, as for a dependency parameter. The unused `build_handler_arguments` and `extract_parameter_value` of `django_bolt._kwargs` are gone.
- **Each exception from an ASGI mount is a 500** - A mount app that raised `HTTPException` or `msgspec.ValidationError` when Bolt called it got the status of that error, such as a 404 or a 422. A mount app that raised the same error in its coroutine got a 500. Now each exception from a mount app gives a 500. An ASGI app sends its own error responses with `http.response.start`. The Rust core no longer reads `HTTPException` or validation errors. Only an exception that `_dispatch` does not catch, or an exception from a mount, reaches it.
- **A response validation 500 hides the validation errors when `DEBUG` is off** - The 500 for a response that fails its type had `extra.validation_errors`. These errors name the model, the fields and the types of the handler. Bolt sent them to the client also with `DEBUG=False`. Now the body is only `{"detail": "Response validation error"}` when `DEBUG=False`. The errors stay in the server log. With `DEBUG=True`, the body keeps `extra.validation_errors`. An `error_formatter` gets an empty `error.errors` for this error when `DEBUG=False`, so a formatter cannot send them by mistake. `error.exception.errors()` still has them.
- **Only a `FileResponse` error is a 404 or a 403** - Bolt answered 404 for each `FileNotFoundError` and 403 for each `PermissionError`. This included an `open()` of handler code, so a server fault looked like a client error. Without an `error_formatter`, the response also showed the server path, also with `DEBUG=False`. Now `FileResponse` raises `ResponseFileNotFound` and `ResponseFileNotAllowed`, which subclass the built-in errors. `File` raises `ResponseFileNotFound` for a missing file. Only these errors give a 404 or a 403, and the response does not show the path. Another `FileNotFoundError` or `PermissionError` gives a 500, and Bolt logs it. A `FileResponse` or `File` path that names no regular file now gives a 404 in place of a 500. Examples are a directory, a path below a file, a name that is too long, a NUL byte and a symlink loop. For these paths, `FileResponse` raises `ResponseFileNotFound` in place of `ValueError` or `OSError`. A path outside `BOLT_ALLOWED_FILE_PATHS` gives a 403, also when the server cannot read it. A file in the allowed directories that the server cannot read gives a logged 500. A handler that raised `FileNotFoundError` or `PermissionError` to get a 404 or a 403 now gets a 500. Raise `NotFound` or `Forbidden` in place of them.
- **Only a server error writes an exception record** - Bolt wrote an ERROR record with a traceback to `django.server` for each exception that a handler did not handle. It also called `exception_logging_handler`. It did this also for a 4xx, such as a 422 for a struct parameter or a JSON body, or a 403 for `PermissionDenied`. A client could cause one on each request. Now Bolt writes the record and calls the handler only for an exception that it answers with a 5xx by default. The access log of `runbolt` records a 4xx at `WARNING`. An `error_formatter` can change the status of the response, but not this choice. When Bolt cannot build the error response, also for an `HTTPException`, it logs that error too. `LoggingMiddleware.log_exception` no longer skips a `SuspiciousOperation`, because Bolt does not call it for the 400.
- **`@cors` with no origin and no pattern raises `ValueError`** - `@cors(origins=[])` used the global CORS settings. Now `@cors` raises `ValueError` when `origins` and `origin_regexes` are both empty. A second cors config on one route also raises `ValueError`, from `@cors` or from `@middleware(type="cors")`. Before, Bolt used only one of them. In a `cors` dict of an API or a router, `origins` and `origin_regexes` take a `str` or a collection of `str`. Another type now stops startup with `TypeError`. Before, Bolt ignored the value, and the routes used the global settings. An empty pattern in the dict stops startup with `ValueError`.
- **The compression middleware copies nothing per request** - For each request, the compression middleware copied the `Accept-Encoding` value to a new string, cloned the shared compression config, and boxed its future. Now it reads the value and the config from the response, which holds its request, after the handler returns. Its future has a named type, so it needs no box. With mimalloc, the allocator of the extension, the middleware runs about 720 instructions for a small JSON response, from 790. A response with the identity marker of `@no_compress` takes about 480 fewer instructions. These counts leave out the atomic clone of the shared config that each request made before, because the probe app has no config.
- **`Accept-Encoding` parsing makes no copies** - Bolt made a lower-case copy of each parameter of each `Accept-Encoding` entry, such as `q=0.6`. It also parsed the parameters of codings that do not change the result. Now it reads the `q` parameter in place, and only for the coding that it checks and for `*`. A browser header such as `gzip, deflate, br, zstd` takes about 710 instructions, from 1150. A header with `q` values takes about 1770, from 5050.
- **A response builds its headers with fewer copies** - Bolt parsed the `Content-Type` header name and copied its value for each response. It also made a lower-case copy of each custom header name. Now the header names are constants, and the content type of a built-in response type is a constant value. The Rust code that builds a JSON response runs about 520 instructions, from 870. With two custom headers, it runs about 1660, from 2330.
- **Request headers are checked once** - Bolt checked that each header value is visible ASCII, and then checked the same bytes again as UTF-8. ASCII is valid UTF-8, so the second check found nothing. Now Bolt skips it. A route that reads headers, or has auth, guards, a rate limit or a form, extracts 14 browser headers in about 7300 instructions, from 8600.
- **A route match borrows its path params** - For each request to a route with path params, Bolt copied the name and the value of each param into a new map. The CORS middleware also built this map for each cross-origin request, and then dropped it. Now the match borrows the names from the router and the values from the path. Bolt copies a value only when it has a `%` escape. A route with one path param gets its route and params in about 1010 instructions, from 1320. The CORS lookup of that route takes about 310, from 1220.
- **The CORS middleware copies less per request** - For each request, the CORS middleware boxed its future. For a cross-origin request, it also copied the origin, the path and the requested method, and cloned the shared app state. Now it reads them from the response, which holds its request, after the handler returns. Its future has a named type, so it needs no box. The middleware runs about 140 instructions for a same-origin request, from 490. A cross-origin request takes about 1700, from 2330.
- **Authentication copies less per request** - Bolt made a new string for the backend name of each authenticated request, and Python made one more. It also copied each permission of a JWT, each string claim and each audience list. Now the backend name is a constant string, and the permissions move out of the claims. The authentication of a JWT and its Python context take about 43200 instructions, from 44000. The HMAC check is about half of that. An API key takes about 6260, from 6610.
- **Response validation is compiled once per route** - Bolt classified the response type on each response. Now it builds the validation for the type when the route registers. Validation of a dict to a `Struct` takes about 285 ns, from 500 ns. A list of 20 model instances takes about 1.8 µs, from 4.2 µs.
- **The client address and scheme cost less per request** - For each request that reads headers, Bolt parsed the names `X-Forwarded-For` and `X-Real-IP` and copied each to a new buffer. It did this also with no trusted proxy, when Bolt ignores these headers. Now the names are constants, and Bolt looks up the headers only for a trusted peer. Bolt also computes the mask of each trusted proxy block once, at startup. With mimalloc and no trusted proxy, the client address takes about 70 instructions, from 850. Behind a trusted proxy with two `X-Forwarded-For` hops, it takes about 1330, from 2520. The scheme from the `X-Forwarded-Proto` of a trusted proxy takes about 460, from 860.
- **A rate limit costs less per request** - For each 429, Bolt made a new clock to compute the wait. That clock runs `cpuid` to select its source, and a virtual machine traps that instruction. Now Bolt reads the clock of the limiter. Bolt also parsed the three 429 header names, and copied the body from a cache behind a lock. Now the names are constants, and the body of a one-second wait is static. The map of limiters now hashes its key with ahash in place of SipHash. In a Firecracker virtual machine, a 429 takes about 2.3 µs, from 25 µs. With mimalloc, an allowed request with `key="ip"` runs about 255 instructions in the limiter, from 545.
- **A rate limit logs one 429 line per second for each bucket** - Bolt wrote a line to stderr for each 429. Under a flood, that was one write for each rejected request, and the log grew without a limit. Now a bucket writes a line for its first 429 in each second. The line ends with `suppressed: N`, the number of 429s of that bucket since its last line that wrote no line.
- **A response object or a Struct result costs less to serialize** - Bolt checked a response object against each response type in turn, and a `Response` was the eighth check. Now Bolt looks up its class. A Struct result went through the bytes, str, stream and QuerySet checks first. Now it takes the JSON path first. An async route also creates fewer coroutines for each response. A `Response` costs about 23% fewer instructions, and a Struct result about 36% fewer.
- **`dump()` of a Serializer with a write-only, computed or nested field costs less** - `dump()` read the field configuration and checked each field for a QuerySet on each call. Now Bolt builds one function for each Serializer class when the class is first ready to dump. `dump()` and `dump_many()` with no options use it. A Serializer with three fields, a write-only field and a computed field dumps in 290 ns instead of 1,070 ns. A `list[Account]` route with 100 items takes 38.5 µs instead of 123 µs. A field that can hold a manager or a QuerySet still raises `SerializationError`.
- **The dispatch of a route with no auth costs less** - Bolt read the auth context of each request, also on a route with no auth backend. Now it reads the context only for a route with a backend. The async dispatch also found the API with the middleware of the route on each request. Now registration stores that API. For a route with no auth, the Python dispatch runs 4% to 14% fewer instructions. A route with auth runs about 3% fewer on the async dispatch, and about 1.5% more on the sync dispatch.
- **A guarded route with no auth backend uses `JWTAuthentication()`** - A route with `guards=[IsAuthenticated()]`, no `auth` and no `BOLT_AUTHENTICATION_CLASSES` had no backend. Thus each request got a 401, also a request with a valid token. Now such a route gets `JWTAuthentication()`, which verifies an HS256 token in the `Authorization` header with `SECRET_KEY`. The rule applies to each guard that needs a user: `IsAuthenticated` and `Requires`, from the route, a router, a class-based view, an `@action`, `BOLT_DEFAULT_PERMISSION_CLASSES`, a WebSocket route or an MCP mount. A route with no guard, or with `AllowAny()` first, still has no backend. `auth=[]` and `BOLT_AUTHENTICATION_CLASSES = []` still mean no backend.

### Removed

- **BREAKING: public names that no Bolt code called** - Bolt removes these names with no deprecation step. Bolt did not call them, and request dispatch did not use them. (#873)
  - `django_bolt.auth`: `register_auth_backend()`, `get_registered_backend()` and `load_user()`. Bolt resolves the user loader of each route at registration. To get the user, use `request.user`, `await request.auser()` or `CurrentUser`.
  - `django_bolt.auth.user_loader`: `load_user_sync()`, `default_django_user_loader()`, `default_django_user_aloader()` and `DEFAULT_USER_LOADERS`. Use `request.user` or `await request.auser()`. To change the query, override `get_user_sync` or `get_user` on the backend.
  - `django_bolt.websocket`: `run_websocket_handler()` and `is_websocket_handler()`. Register a handler with `@api.websocket()`.
  - `django_bolt.admin`: `get_admin_route_patterns()` and `get_admin_info()`. Bolt mounts the admin site as an ASGI app. Use `is_admin_installed()`, `should_enable_admin()` and `detect_admin_url_prefix()`.
  - `django_bolt.admin.admin_detection.get_static_url_prefix()`. Read `settings.STATIC_URL`.
  - The `host` and `port` arguments of `AdminRouteRegistrar.register_routes()`. They had no effect. Call `register_routes()` with no arguments.
  - `django_bolt.request.is_request()`. No replacement: the `request` argument of a handler is always a Bolt request.
  - `Response.to_bytes()`. `JSON`, `PlainText` and `HTML` keep `to_bytes()`.
  - `django_bolt.openapi.spec.schema.SchemaDataContainer`. No replacement.
- **Three definitions that nothing used** - `django_bolt.responses.UploadFile` was an old copy of the upload class. The public `UploadFile` is `django_bolt.datastructures.UploadFile`, and `from django_bolt import UploadFile` still gives it. `django_bolt.testing.helpers.create_test_client` was a wrapper of `TestClient(...)` that `django_bolt.testing` did not export. `django_bolt._json.decode_typed` had no caller. Use `TestClient(api)` and `msgspec.json.decode(value, type=...)`.
- **Definitions and branches that nothing reached** - `APIView.initialize()` was a hook that Bolt never called. Route metadata had a `path_params` key that nothing read. `django_bolt._kwargs.create_file_extractor()` took `max_size`, `min_size`, `allowed_types` and `max_files` and ignored them: Rust checks these limits from the route metadata. `Serializer` had the class attributes `__default_values_map__` and `__field_marker_defaults__`, and `subset()` set `__parent_serializer__` and `__subset_fields__`: nothing read them. `get_msgspec_type_for_django_field()` had branches for `EmailField`, `URLField`, `SlugField` and `OneToOneField` after the branches of their base classes. `WebSocketTestClient` had a path for a route with no injector, but each WebSocket route has one. `check_database()` had an answer for an installation with no Django. `UploadFile` had an error for a file with no content and no temp path, which no constructor of `UploadFile` makes. No behavior changes.
- **`LoggingConfig` options with no effect on the access log** - `request_log_fields`, `response_log_fields`, `obfuscate_headers`, `obfuscate_cookies`, `log_request_body`, `log_response_body`, `max_body_log_size` and `log_level` did not change the access log of `runbolt`. The Rust line has a fixed format: method, path, status and duration. Now `LoggingConfig` and `create_logging_middleware()` raise `TypeError` for these options, and for each other unknown option. Remove the option. It had no effect on the access log. To change the level of the access log, set the level of its logger in `LOGGING`. (#866)
- **`LoggingMiddleware.log_request()`, `log_response()`, `extract_request_data()`, `obfuscate_headers()` and `obfuscate_cookies()`** - Bolt did not call these methods. The Rust server writes the access log. Remove the calls. To log more fields of a request, use a middleware of your own. `log_exception()` stays. (#866)

### Security

- **actix-http has the fixes for three advisories** - The server now uses actix-http 3.22.0. It fixes RUSTSEC-2026-0552: a bare LF in chunked trailers can consume a pipelined request or stop body decoding. It fixes RUSTSEC-2026-0553: a bad quoted chunk extension can end an HTTP/1.1 request body early. It fixes RUSTSEC-2026-0554: a partial request on a keep-alive connection can bypass the request header timeout. `Cargo.toml` now requires actix-http 3.21.3 or later.
- **Actix Web has the fixes for five advisories** - The server now uses actix-web 4.18.2, actix-http 3.20.3 and actix-files 0.7.4. These versions fix RUSTSEC-2026-0446, a request smuggling attack through bad chunked trailers. They also fix three ways to use all memory with a compressed request body: RUSTSEC-2026-0447, RUSTSEC-2026-0448 and RUSTSEC-2026-0455. RUSTSEC-2026-0443 is also fixed: a Windows short name showed a hidden file. `Cargo.toml` now requires these versions or later.
- **Bolt requires Django 5.2.19 or later** - Django 5.2.19, 6.0.10 and 6.1.3 fix six CVEs. Examples are memory exhaustion in `MultiPartParser` and password hashes that a view-only admin user can see. Upgrade Django to the latest patch of your series.
- **A declared response type hides the write-only fields of each Serializer in it** - Bolt dumped a Serializer only when the response type was the Serializer, an optional Serializer or a list of Serializers. msgspec encodes each other Serializer with all of its fields. Thus a route with `-> Envelope`, where `Envelope` is a Struct with an `Account` field, sent the write-only fields of the `Account`, for example a password. The computed fields were missing. `dict[str, Account]`, `tuple[Account, ...]`, a TypedDict, a dataclass field, a union and a generic Struct such as `Page[Account]` had the same problem. Now Bolt gives the `dump()` of each Serializer at each position of the declared type. `dump()` also dumps a Serializer in a Struct, dict, tuple or union field of a Serializer. Before, it dumped only a field of a Serializer type or a list of Serializers. This applies to the return annotation, to each type of `response_model={code: type}`, and to the item type of a typed stream. It applies also when the route does not validate its response. Bolt makes the plan of the type once, when the route registers. A type with no Serializer gets no plan, so its route costs the same as before. A Struct route with a Serializer keeps the inline encode path. A type that names a class that the module defines later gets its plan at the first response. A value with no declared type, such as `-> dict` or a return with no annotation, goes out as msgspec encodes it. To hide the write-only fields there, declare the type or call `dump()`.

## [0.13.0]

### Added

- **`BoltAPI(error_formatter=...)`** - One function formats each error response that the framework makes. It covers the errors of the Rust core. These are 401 and 403 from guards, 404 for no route, 413, and 400 for bad headers. They also include 422 from request values and 429 from rate limits. It also covers a `FileResponse` whose file Rust cannot open or read. It also covers the Python errors: request and response validation, `HTTPException` and unhandled exceptions. The function gets a `BoltError` with `status`, a stable `kind`, `detail`, the field `errors`, `headers`, `extra` and `exception`, and the request. It returns a `Response`, `JSON`, `PlainText` or `HTML`. Bolt adds the error headers, so a 401 keeps `WWW-Authenticate` and a 429 keeps `Retry-After`. `django_bolt.error_handlers.problem_details` formats RFC 9457 Problem Details. Rust calls Python only for an error, and only when the app has a formatter. Without a formatter, the responses do not change. `runbolt` stops when two APIs of a project set different formatters, and `mount()` raises `ValueError`. An `HTTPException` with a typed body keeps its body. ASGI and MCP mounts keep their own error responses. An async formatter raises `TypeError`. A formatter response with a status out of 400 to 599, or a header that is not valid, gives the default response. Bolt then logs the error. See [Error formatter](docs/src/topics/error-handling.md#write-a-formatter).
- **The OpenAPI schema shows the error body of `error_formatter`** - With `problem_details`, the generated 422 responses use `application/problem+json` and the `ProblemDetails` schema, with `errors`. Decorate your own formatter with `error_schema(Type, media_type=...)` to show its body. A formatter without `error_schema` gets a 422 with no content, because Bolt cannot know its body. `OpenAPIConfig(strict=True)` reports such a formatter. An `"errors"` key in `error.extra` does not change the `errors` list of `problem_details`. Without a formatter, the schema does not change. See [OpenAPI schema](docs/src/topics/error-handling.md#write-a-formatter).
- **`SessionAuthentication`** - A route can use Django session auth as it uses a token backend: `auth=[SessionAuthentication()]`. Django does the session work. Bolt adds Django's `SessionMiddleware` and `AuthenticationMiddleware` to the route when its middleware has neither. When it has only one of them, startup fails. Rust rejects a request with no session cookie. For a request with the cookie, Django loads the user, and Bolt checks the guards of the route against `request.user`. A guard can read `is_staff`, `is_superuser` and `permissions` of the user. A `none_of` guard on another claim is an error at startup. `login_url` sends an anonymous user to a login page with Django's `redirect_to_login`. Each unsafe request to the route gets the CSRF origin check of a JWT cookie, also with no cookie, so a login route is protected too. `csrf=False` turns the check off. A token backend on the same route comes first. The backend works on HTTP routes only. A WebSocket route or an MCP mount with it in `auth` raises an error at startup. A session backend from `BOLT_AUTHENTICATION_CLASSES` is left out there. See [Session authentication](docs/src/topics/authentication.md#authenticate-with-a-django-session).
- **`pool` extra** - `pip install "django-bolt[pool]"` installs psycopg with psycopg_pool 3.3 or later. Bolt drains a pool after a database restart with `drain()`, which psycopg_pool adds in 3.3. The extra does not install libpq. Install libpq on the host, or install `psycopg[binary]`. See [Database connections](docs/src/topics/deployment.md#configure-database-connections).

### Changed

- **A multipart part header over 64 KB gets 422** - Bolt now uses actix-multipart 0.8. Its parser keeps at most 64 KB of data that it cannot use yet. Thus a part header that is longer than 64 KB now gets 422 with the type `multipart_error`. Before, Bolt kept such a header up to `BOLT_MAX_UPLOAD_SIZE` and accepted it. A browser does not send such a header.
- **A handler with only the request parameter takes the sync dispatch** - Bolt sent a handler such as `def view(request)` through an asyncio task on the worker loop, also when it could not suspend. Now a sync handler that does not block, and an async handler with no `await`, run directly from Rust, as handlers with typed parameters do. This saves about 14 µs for each request. On one core, `def view(request)` went from 45.7 µs to 31.3 µs. Such a handler now runs with no current asyncio task. Such a sync handler also has no running event loop, as other sync handlers on this path. A QuerySet that such a handler returns still loads on the ORM thread pool.
- **A pooled connection goes back to its pool when its thread has no more work** - With Django's connection pool, each thread kept its connection between requests. Idle ORM threads, executor threads and lanes each held a pool slot, and a burst could start more threads than `max_size`. Then requests waited for the pool `timeout` and failed with `PoolTimeout`, also after the burst. Measured on 2 processes with `max_size` 16 and a 10 s network partition: 1160 requests failed. The server did not recover in the 30 s after the partition. Now 18 requests fail, all in its first 0.3 s, and the requests that wait during the partition complete when it ends. Now a worker of a Bolt thread pool gives its pooled connection back when its queue is empty. Calls that wait in its queue use the same connection, so throughput under load does not change. A lane gives the connection back at the end of each request, and a sync streaming body when it ends. `max_size` now limits the queries in flight, not the threads. The check before each call leaves pooled databases out, because the pool checks its own connections. A worker that was idle checks out a connection again, for about 20 to 30 µs. A pool `check` callback, which `CONN_HEALTH_CHECKS` also adds, then runs on each checkout, so Bolt logs a warning at startup when a pool has one. See [Database connections](docs/src/topics/deployment.md#configure-database-connections).
- **A Django setting with the wrong type stops startup** - Bolt used the default when a setting had the wrong type, and gave no error. For example, `BOLT_MAX_UPLOAD_SIZE = os.environ["MAX_UPLOAD"]` is a str, so a 2 MB upload got 413 although the setting said 10 MB. Now `runbolt` and `TestClient` raise `ImproperlyConfigured` that names the setting. A missing setting still gives the default. Bolt checks these settings:
  - `CORS_ALLOW_ALL_ORIGINS` and `CORS_ALLOW_CREDENTIALS` must be a bool.
  - `DEBUG` must be a bool or an int. Bolt reads an int as Django does: `0` is off, and another int is on. A str such as `"False"` stops startup, because Django reads it as on. Before, Bolt used `False` for `DEBUG = 1` and for `DEBUG = "True"`.
  - `BOLT_MAX_UPLOAD_SIZE`, `BOLT_MAX_HEADER_SIZE`, `BOLT_MEMORY_SPOOL_THRESHOLD`, `BOLT_WS_MAX_CONNECTIONS`, `BOLT_WS_CLIENT_TIMEOUT` and `BOLT_WS_MAX_MESSAGE_SIZE` must be an int of 0 or more. A bool is not an int.
  - `BOLT_STATIC_MAX_AGE` and `BOLT_MEDIA_MAX_AGE` must be an int of 0 or more, or `None`. Before, a wrong value gave a warning, and static and media files had no `Cache-Control`.
  - `BOLT_MAX_SYNC_STREAMING_THREADS`, `BOLT_WS_CHANNEL_SIZE` and `BOLT_WS_HEARTBEAT_INTERVAL` must be an int of 1 or more.
